- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/zhang_exact_speedups.cpp::binom_two_sided_pval`, `R/EDI/src/zhang_exact_speedups.cpp::zhang_exact_binom_pval_cpp`
- **Sources:** https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wch/r-source/trunk/src/library/stats/R/binom.test.R

### exact-rank-null-distribution: exhaustive-enumeration → Implement the already-Adopted level DP over (level k, treated_so_far) -> distribution of partial 2U, but compute only the two tail masses. Precompute for each (k, remaining treated r) the min and max achievable remaining 2U contribution, filled level by level with a greedy or DP. Any node whose full reachable 2U interval lies at or below obs, or at or above obs, has its whole mass added to that tail in closed form: the product of log-choose weights times C(n_rest, r)/C(n, n_T). The node is not expanded. This is Cheung & Klotz (1997) with Klotz's twoUmin/twoUmax bounds, as coded in golang.org/x/perf internal/stats/udist.go, and the network-algorithm tail-pruning idea (Mehta-Patel family). With exact polynomial cost, the 1e6-allocation guard could be replaced by a cell-count guard sized for the DP.
- **Current:** DFS over every composition (t_1..t_K) builds the full PMF of 2U. Above 1e6 allocations a guard refuses and points to asymptotic inference, so there is no exact answer for large K (an 0-10 scale with n=100-200 has 4.5e9-2.8e12 leaves).
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by any source. udist.go notes only that its map-based version spends ~60% of time in hashing, and that slices could roughly double the speed. Theory: the polynomial DP removes the exponential blow-up (catalog: 2.8e12 leaves -> ~4e8 cell updates at K=11, n=200). Tail pruning then cuts the number of expanded partial-2U values per node to the band compatible with obs, which is a large saving when obs is in the tail (small p-values) and little when obs is near the center. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** The PMF is equal to tolerance, not bit-identical, because summation order changes. Pruning gives tail sums only, not the PMF; EDI uses only p_lower and p_upper, so this is fine. The two-sided p needs both tails, so the bounds must handle the obs tie case (2U == obs counts in both tails). The code is more complex than the plain dense DP; the gain at small K is negligible, so keep enumeration for small leaf counts as TODO 1 already says. The finding is also about availability, not only speed: it restores exact p-values that the 2026-11-16 guard now refuses.
- **Suggested action:** Fold this into the catalog §6 TODO 1 (JT exact null -> polynomial DP), as the 'tail-only with reachable-range bounds' variant. Validate against brute-force enumeration at n<=12 and against the current kernel to 1e-12. Use udist.go (BSD-3, attribution) as a reference implementation or test oracle for tied Mann-Whitney CDFs. Replace the 1e6-allocation refusal with a DP-cell budget.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_jonckheere_terpstra.cpp::recurse_jt_distribution`, `R/EDI/src/fast_jonckheere_terpstra.cpp::exact_jonckheere_terpstra_pval_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/fast_jonckheere_terpstra.cpp::jt_exact_leaf_count_exceeds_limit`
- **Sources:** https://raw.githubusercontent.com/golang/perf/master/internal/stats/udist.go https://raw.githubusercontent.com/golang/perf/master/LICENSE https://www3.stat.sinica.edu.tw/statistica/j7n3/j7n316/j7n316.htm https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.05741 https://cran.r-project.org/package=exactRankTests

### gamma-family-functions: continued-fraction-lentz, power-series → Add a fast path for integer or half-integer a = df/2 using the exact finite sums: DLMF 8.4.10, Q(n+1,x)=e^{-x}Σ_{k≤n}x^k/k!, and DLMF 8.4.14, Q(n+½,z²)=erfc(z)+e^{-z²}/√π·Σ_{k=1}^{n} z^{2k−1}/(½)_k. df=1 becomes fast_erfc(√(T/2)) and df=2 becomes exp(−T/2). Keep the series/CF path for non-integer a and for a≥30, as Boost does.
- **Current:** fast_pchisq_upper(T, df) → fast_gammaq(df/2, T/2). This takes the series path (1−P) for x<a+1 and the modified-Lentz continued fraction otherwise, each with up to 500 iterations and an exp(−x + a·log x − fast_lgamma(a)) prefactor. df is always an integer at every call site.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. Boost.Math says these sums are 'more stable and more efficient than the continued fraction alternative' for a<30 but gives no timing; its accuracy table reports 0 eps on integer/half-integer cases against Rmath's 138 eps max. For df=1 the change swaps an iterative loop plus lgamma/log/exp for one rational erfc. End-to-end gain is likely negligible because each p-value follows a model refit; the main benefit is accuracy. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Same quantity (exact identity), deterministic. The gain is mostly accuracy and robustness: no lgamma prefactor cancellation, no iteration cap. The speed gain per call is real but small relative to callers. It adds a branch on whether df is an integer and needs a cross-check against R::pchisq(lower=FALSE). The finite sum for even df at large x needs care to avoid overflow (accumulate the terms in scaled form).
- **Suggested action:** Re-look, low priority (unchanged from last run). Add the df=1 → fast_erfc(√(T/2)) and df=2 → exp(−T/2) special cases, then the general integer/half-integer finite sums for a<30. Validate against R::pchisq(lower=FALSE) for df ∈ {1..60} and T ∈ [1e-8, 1e3]. Can be folded into the row-O accuracy TODO.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_gamma_functions.h::fast_pchisq_upper`, `R/EDI/src/fast_gamma_functions.h::fast_gammaq`, `R/EDI/src/fast_gamma_functions.h::fast_gammaq_cf`, `R/EDI/src/fast_gamma_functions.h::fast_gammap_series`
- **Sources:** https://dlmf.nist.gov/8.4 https://www.boost.org/doc/libs/release/libs/math/doc/html/math_toolkit/sf_gamma/igamma.html https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06434

### gauss-hermite-nodes: golub-welsch → Build one shared, cached rule (TODO-1, already Adopt), and build it the SciPy/NumPy way. Take nodes as eigenvalues only of the tridiagonal Jacobi matrix (computeFromTridiagonal with EigenvaluesOnly, or compute(J, EigenvaluesOnly)), apply one Newton step on H_n, and compute weights from the closed form w_k ∝ 1/(H_{n-1}(x_k)·H_n'(x_k)), using normalised Hermite recurrences in log space and normalised to sum 1. Then symmetrise nodes and weights as lme4 2.0-6 does.
- **Current:** A dense Eigen SelfAdjointEigenSolver (with eigenvectors) on the n×n Jacobi matrix. The log-weights are log(√π·v_{0k}²) − ½log π, taken from the first eigenvector components. It is rebuilt on every data construction (n_gh = 60 by default in fast_poisson_glmm, 20 elsewhere, 7 in hurdle) and copied in 6 places.
- **Claimed speedup:** The rule is built once per fit, so the speed gain is small. For the eigensolve, Eigen documents about 4n³/3 without eigenvectors against about 9n³ with them (≈6.7×), and computeFromTridiagonal skips the reduction as well. The O(n²) Newton/weight pass is cheaper still. Neither source benchmarks this end to end, so the speedup is not quantified. The main gain is accuracy: tail log-weights keep relative accuracy, while Golub–Welsch weights from eigenvector components carry only absolute accuracy, so the smallest weights lose relative accuracy (Gil–Segura–Temme, arXiv 2509.16716). This matters for n_gh = 60, where the outer weights are far below machine epsilon relative to the largest. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** This is a robustness and accuracy finding more than a speed one. The values change in the last bits for central weights and possibly a lot for extreme tail log-weights, so glmer-equivalence and frozen-reference tests may move slightly. Whether the tail error changes any EDI fit is not quantified: it matters only when the integrand puts mass at the outer nodes (non-adaptive GH with large σ or extreme cluster counts in the Poisson GLMM). The result stays deterministic. The approach is a reimplementation of standard formulas: SciPy is BSD-3 and NumPy is BSD-licensed, so both are fine as references.
- **Suggested action:** When doing TODO-1 (a single cached shared GH rule, deduping all 6 copies), build it from eigenvalues only plus a Newton polish, and take log-weights from the recurrence formula, not from log(v0²). Symmetrise the rule. As a check, compare the log-weights at n = 60 and 100 against a high-precision reference (e.g. Gil–Segura–Temme GHi, or mpmath) before and after the change.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/_glmm_engine.h::gauss_hermite_rule`, `R/EDI/src/fast_poisson_glmm.cpp::gauss_hermite_rule_poisson`, `R/EDI/src/fast_logistic_glmm.cpp::gauss_hermite_rule_log`, `R/EDI/src/fast_clogit_plus_glmm.cpp::gauss_hermite_rule`, `R/EDI/src/fast_hurdle_poisson_glmm.cpp::gauss_hermite_rule_hp`, `R/EDI/src/fast_weibull_frailty.cpp::gauss_hermite_rule_wf`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.16716 https://arxiv.org/html/2509.16716 https://raw.githubusercontent.com/scipy/scipy/main/scipy/special/_orthogonal.py https://docs.scipy.org/doc/scipy/reference/generated/scipy.special.roots_hermite.html https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/generated/numpy.polynomial.hermite.hermgauss.html https://libeigen.gitlab.io/eigen/docs-nightly/classEigen_1_1SelfAdjointEigenSolver.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/lme4/news.html https://raw.githubusercontent.com/JuliaApproximation/FastGaussQuadrature.jl/master/src/gausshermite.jl

### gaussian-lmm-fit: lbfgs, sherman-morrison → Sufficient-statistic (Gram-block) evaluation. With group sizes in {1,2} and per-group weights, the ML likelihood depends on the data only through three weighted (p+1)×(p+1) cross-product blocks of Z=[X y]: singletons Σw z zᵀ, pair rows Σw z zᵀ, and pair sums Σw s sᵀ (s = z_1+z_2). Equivalently, a sum/difference (Helmert) rotation of each pair gives three independent variance strata. Precompute these once in O(n·p²). Then ΣwQ_g and ΣwS_g² are quadratic forms in (β,−1), and each NLL+gradient costs O(p²), each profiled-λ evaluation (GLS β̂(λ) = solve of a weighted sum of the blocks) costs O(p³), and neither depends on n. For numerical stability, keep the triangular factors R_k of each stratum's [X_k y_k] (QR) rather than raw Grams, so the residual sum of squares is ‖R_k[β;−1]‖² without cancellation. In `fit_null` the blocks are invariant in δ (fixing β_T just partitions them), so one precompute serves the whole CI search.
- **Current:** Joint L-BFGS over (β, log σ_e, log σ_b). Every NLL+gradient call loops over all n sorted rows to form residuals and the per-group S_g and Q_g, at O(n·p) per call × ~20–100 calls per fit. Constrained refits (`fit_null` over a δ grid for CI inversion; parametric-bootstrap refits) redo this from scratch with the same X and y.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by any source. By operation count, a fit drops from iters·O(n·p) to O(n·p²)+iters·O(p²), roughly min(iters, n/p)-fold. For a δ-grid CI search with K constrained refits it drops from K·iters·O(n·p) to O(n·p²)+K·iters·O(p²). Pichler et al. (arXiv 2502.04990) report the sufficient-statistics advantage growing with n for random-intercept LMMs, but in a Bayesian/Stan setting, not this ML kernel. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** This is the same ML estimator: the change is an algebraic regrouping, so results agree up to rounding but are not bitwise identical. Raw-Gram quadratic forms (c − 2βᵀb + βᵀAβ) can lose precision when residuals are small relative to y; the QR/R-factor form or centering at OLS β avoids that. The precompute must be redone per Bayesian-bootstrap weight vector and per resampled X. It still costs only one O(n·p²) pass per replicate, the same as the existing GLS fast path, so the win is smallest for single estimate-only replicates (already GLS) and largest for full and constrained refits. It is deterministic, needs no new library, and pairs naturally with the existing Prototype (profiled 1-D Brent on λ, catalog T7 / v1.0.5 TODO-3), which also gains robustness at σ_b=0.
- **Suggested action:** Fold into the v1.0.5 TODO-3 profiled-ML prototype: build the three per-stratum R factors (or Grams) once per dataset/weight vector. Evaluate the NLL and gradient (and the profiled λ objective) from them, reuse them across all `fit_null` δ values, and A/B against the current `neg_ll_and_grad` for agreement (≤1e-8 relative) and for timing at n ∈ {50, 500, 5000}, p ∈ {2, 5, 10}.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_gaussian_lmm.cpp::fast_gaussian_lmm_internal`, `R/EDI/src/fast_gaussian_lmm.cpp::neg_ll_and_grad`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.04990 https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.20796 https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.10300 https://ideas.repec.org/a/eee/econom/v41y1989i3p341-361.html https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11674

### gee-fit: irls → Cache the estimated LOR structure, keyed on (y, pairing m), and pass it back as LORstr='fixed' with LORterm (built with multgee::matrixLOR). According to the multgee reference manual, the structure comes from responses aggregated across clusters into marginalized tables, so covariates do not enter it. It is therefore unchanged across ladder rungs, and across permutation draws whenever y and m are fixed.
- **Current:** Each fit_ordinal_gee_mod call runs multgee::ordLORgee(LORstr='uniform'). That re-estimates the uniform local-odds-ratio structure, an RC-G(1) association model on marginalized contingency tables, on every ladder rung and every randomization draw.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by any source. It removes one association-model fit per ordLORgee call; what share of the call that is remains unknown. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Gives identical coefficients only if the cached structure is exactly what LORstr='uniform' would re-estimate; this needs an A/B check. The cache must be invalidated whenever y or the pairing changes. Adds code coupling to multgee internals (the LORterm layout).
- **Suggested action:** Profile one ordLORgee call at typical KK sizes to split the time between LOR estimation and Fisher scoring. If LOR estimation is a material share, prototype the cache and A/B it against current fits for coefficients identical within tolerance.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_ordinal_KK_combined.R::fit_ordinal_gee_mod`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/multgee/refman/multgee.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=multgee

### generic-smooth-mle-optimization: newton-raphson → A gradient-regularized (Levenberg–Marquardt-type) Newton step: solve (H + λ_k I) s = −g with λ_k ∝ sqrt(‖g_k‖), using the AN2C / Mishchenko rule, and add a [−λ_min(H)]_+ shift when LLT/LDLT detects indefiniteness. Factor with LLT/LDLT instead of FullPivLU. Keep step-halving and the L-BFGS fallback as the outer safety net. This gives a concrete, parameter-free τ for catalog §1 TODO-9.
- **Current:** Damped Newton on the observed Hessian, solved by FullPivLU. There is no definiteness or descent-direction check, and the step is halved to 1e-4. On failure the fit hands off to L-BFGS from Newton's endpoint. warm_start_hessian is ignored.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified relative to EDI's loop. On 119 small OPM/CUTEst problems (2–49 variables), Gratton–Jerad–Toint (arXiv 2302.10065) report parity with trust region and AR2 in iteration-profile area (AN2CE 0.91, TR2M 0.91), not a win. In the marqLevAlg R Journal paper, LM-damped Newton took 6–24 iterations against 52–502 for BFGS and 244–823 for L-BFGS-B on a 17–18-parameter joint model, with similar wall-clock, and it converged consistently where the others depended on scaling. The expected gain is robustness: fewer wasted halving cascades (~14 evaluations each) and fewer L-BFGS hand-offs. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Mainly a robustness finding. On strictly concave fits the MLE is unchanged within tolerance. On non-concave NB, beta and stereotype surfaces the iterate path changes, so a different local optimum is possible; this needs the A/B harness. There is no speedup on easy fits. The finding is deterministic and adds no license issue, since it is self-written code using Eigen.
- **Suggested action:** When prototyping catalog §1 TODO-9, use the √‖g‖ regularization with an LLT/LDLT factorization and the indefiniteness shift. Either honour or remove warm_start_hessian. Measure halving cascades, L-BFGS hand-offs and wall-clock on NB, beta and stereotype in the A/B harness.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::optimize_likelihood_newton`, `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::optimize_likelihood_newton_then_lbfgs`, `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::optimize_likelihood`, `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::optimize_fixed_likelihood`, `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::optimize_fixed_likelihood_newton`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2302.10065 https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04807 https://doi.org/10.1137/22M1488752 https://journal.r-project.org/articles/RJ-2021-089
- **Open copies** (publisher not usable): https://arxiv.org/abs/2112.02089

### glmm-marginal-likelihood: gauss-hermite-quadrature, louis-identity → Cluster-total (sufficient-statistic) collapse for the log link. Per group, form S_g=sum w*y, log Lambda_g=LSE(log w+eta) and A_g=sum w(y*eta-log y!). Each node term is log w_k+A_g+b_k*S_g-exp(b_k+log Lambda_g), which costs O(G*K). The beta-gradient is one GEMV X'[w(y-e^eta*E_g[e^b])]. The Louis beta-beta block is one weighted crossprod with weights w*e^eta*E_g[e^b], and the cross/E[GG'] terms come from u_g=X_g'wy, v_g=X_g'we^eta and posterior moments of e^b and b. This is the same quadrature-approximated likelihood algebraically, with only the summation order changed. The hurdle kernel already shares e^eta*e^b_k.
- **Current:** 60-node non-adaptive GH. Every evaluation computes exp(eta_i+b_k) for all n rows at every node (fast_poisson_glmm.cpp:199-200, :258-259), and the Louis Hessian does one weighted_crossprod over all n per node (:276-283). Cost O(n*K*p) per gradient and O(n*K*p^2) per Hessian.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by any source; this is a derivation from the code. The n-dependent work drops from O(n*K*p) to O(n*p)+O(G*K) per evaluation. Expect roughly 3-10x on gradient+Hessian at K=60, p=5-20, G of about n/2. Must be measured. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** A rewrite of operator(), hessian and the score/Hessian exports, with summation-order differences of about 1e-12. It must A/B against the current kernel on the R8 grid. Row weights must be carried into S_g, Lambda_g and A_g (the weighted pseudo-likelihood stays exact because the weights are per row inside the group sum). Deterministic.
- **Suggested action:** Add a v1.0.5 catalog section 4 Adopt item for fast_poisson_glmm.cpp (cluster-total form for operator(), hessian and the :515/:545 exports). Gate: the existing tests plus an all.equal(tol 1e-10) A/B on the R8 grid, then benchmark at n=100-1000.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_poisson_glmm.cpp::PoissonGLMMObjective`, `R/EDI/src/fast_poisson_glmm.cpp::hessian`, `R/EDI/R/inference_count_KK_combined.R::shared_rcpp`, `R/EDI/R/inference_count_KK_combined.R::compute_estimate_with_bootstrap_weights`, `R/EDI/R/inference_count_KK_combined.R::compute_treatment_estimate_during_randomization_inference`
- **Sources:** https://ageconsearch.umn.edu/record/263790/files/canterbury-nz-098.pdf https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.01589

### glmm-marginal-likelihood: gauss-hermite-quadrature, lbfgs → Adaptive GH with fewer nodes, by one of two routes. (a) The exact AQ gradient via implicit differentiation of the per-group mode, keeping L-BFGS plus one Newton polish (Stringer arXiv 2310.01589 v2, no journal DOI). (b) Pseudo-adaptive GH: nodes centred and scaled once from an initial fit and then frozen, so the cheap fixed-node analytic gradient still applies (Rizopoulos 2012, CSDA 56(3):491-501, doi 10.1016/j.csda.2011.09.007).
- **Current:** Fixed non-adaptive GH (20 nodes in logistic/clogit/engine, 7 in hurdle) with the exact fixed-node gradient and L-BFGS.
- **Claimed speedup:** Stringer: faster than finite-difference (GLMMadaptive) and derivative-free (lme4) approaches. The abstract does not quantify the gain; the paper's text reports about 2-4x versus those packages, not versus EDI. Rizopoulos: 'considerably faster than the standard Gauss-Hermite rule', not quantified on the abstract page. Versus EDI's 20 fixed nodes at m_g<=2, catalog T1 estimates 1.2-1.6x. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Changes results (a different quadrature rule), so it needs an A/B with an equivalence metric against a high-K reference. The main gain is accuracy at large sigma: Stringer, Bilodeau & Tang (arXiv 2202.07864 v2, 2025) prove that non-adaptive-quadrature MLEs are inconsistent under weak conditions. Speed gain at m_g<=2 is probably small.
- **Suggested action:** Keep under v1.1.0 performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md TODO-153. Add arXiv 2202.07864 as the accuracy argument and the Rizopoulos DOI as the frozen-mode route. Prioritise the logistic/clogit 20-node kernels and the hurdle K=7 accuracy item (v1.0.5 catalog TODO 5); the Poisson kernel is lower priority once the cluster-total rewrite lands.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_logistic_glmm.cpp::LogisticGLMMObjective`, `R/EDI/src/fast_logistic_glmm.cpp::value`, `R/EDI/src/fast_clogit_plus_glmm.cpp::neg_glmm`, `R/EDI/src/fast_clogit_plus_glmm.cpp::fast_clogit_plus_glmm_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/_glmm_engine.h::GLMMObjective`, `R/EDI/src/_glmm_engine.h::value`, `R/EDI/src/fast_hurdle_poisson_glmm.cpp::HurdlePoissonGLMMObjective`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.01589 https://ideas.repec.org/a/eee/csdana/v56y2012i3p491-501.html https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.07864

### gram-matrix-accumulation: weighted-gram-accumulation → (a) Minimal: annotate both inner loops with EDI_SIMD_REDUCTION(+:acc,score_acc), or form w∘x_j once per j and use Eigen's dot, as the col-major weighted_crossprod already does. Eigen's packet reduction uses SIMD lanes and several accumulators. (b) Fuller: a register-blocked tile kernel like tabmat's dense sandwich (dense_helpers.hpp). It packs a k-block of rows, scales one panel by w, computes 4×4 output tiles with up to 16 SIMD FMA accumulators over the lower triangle only, and mirrors at the end. That cuts loads per FMA by about 4× and vectorises. The score X'r can be fused as an extra column. Run the k-blocks in fixed serial order (tabmat's column/row-parallel mode), not its atomic k-parallel mode, to stay deterministic.
- **Current:** Fused score + X'WX on the logistic and probit IRLS hot path, run once per iteration per resampling replicate. For each upper-triangle column pair (j,k) there is a scalar loop over i with one double accumulator (`acc += xj[i]*w[i]*xk[i]`), with no `omp simd reduction` / EDI_SIMD_REDUCTION hint and no -ffast-math in Makevars. Strict FP semantics forbid the reassociation that vectorising a reduction needs, so GCC and clang keep these loops scalar: about one dependent add per FP-add latency. Each pair also re-streams three length-n arrays (x_j, w, x_k), about 1.5·n·p² loads in total.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by any source at EDI's sizes. The basis is mechanism, not a benchmark: SIMD width (4 doubles with AVX2, 8 with AVX-512) plus multiple accumulators that break the add-latency chain. glum/tabmat describe their dense sandwich as 'parallelized, cache-friendly, SIMD-optimized' but publish no standalone dense number. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Summation order changes, so X'WX and the score differ from today's at the rounding level (about 1e-16 relative). Converged β should agree within IRLS tolerance, but any bit-for-bit regression snapshots would need refreshing. Results stay deterministic for a fixed build, ISA and any thread count, because the kernel is serial within a fit. They can differ across ISAs (AVX2 vs AVX-512 lane counts), as Eigen's dot path already can. Option (a) is a few lines. Option (b) adds a packed n_block×p buffer and more code to test. On CRAN macOS without OpenMP, EDI_SIMD_REDUCTION maps to clang's assume_safety hint, so (a) still applies there. Plain GCC without OpenMP drops the hint, so the Eigen-dot route is the more portable form of (a). At p ≤ 3 the gain is small either way.
- **Suggested action:** Fold into performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md TODO-155 as a cheap first step before the √w∘X + DSYRK option. Check vectorisation with -fopt-info-vec-missed on _helper_functions_core.h users (targeted compile of fast_logistic_regression.cpp only). Then microbenchmark score_weighted_crossprod_colwise_assign against an EDI_SIMD_REDUCTION version and an Eigen-dot version at n ∈ {100, 500, 2000}, p ∈ {2, 5, 10, 20}, and confirm the β agreement tolerance in the logistic and probit tests.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::score_weighted_crossprod_colwise_assign`
- **Sources:** https://lists.llvm.org/pipermail/llvm-dev/2020-June/142764.html https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Quantco/tabmat/main/src/tabmat/ext/dense_helpers.hpp https://glum.readthedocs.io/en/stable/motivation.html https://github.com/Quantco/tabmat/blob/main/LICENSE

### greedy-swap-allocation-search: fisher-yates-shuffle → Batched ranged random integer generation (Brackett-Rozinsky & Lemire): several unbiased bounded indices from one random word, so fewer RNG calls per shuffle. Alternatively, a partial shuffle of only min(n_T, n−n_T) positions.
- **Current:** Explicit Durstenfeld Fisher-Yates via edi_rng::fisher_yates_shuffle (one bounded integer per position) over RRng, re-iota'd per draw.
- **Claimed speedup:** The paper reports 1.4–2.6× on whole shuffles with 64-bit PCG/Lehmer generators. It was not measured with MT19937 or 32-bit words. In this problem the shuffle is a negligible share of the per-draw cost, since the swap search dominates, so the end-to-end gain is not quantified and is likely near 1×. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Same distribution, unbiased and deterministic, but it changes the seed-pinned stream, so fixtures must be refreshed. Benefit within greedy/D/A searches is marginal because the O(n_T n_C) swap rounds dominate.
- **Suggested action:** Keep it as a recorded option in catalog P2. Adopt it only together with another planned RNG-stream change, so seeds are refreshed once. Do not prioritise it for this problem.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/optimal_design_search.cpp::random_balanced_init`
- **Sources:** https://doi.org/10.1002/spe.3369 https://github.com/lemire/batched_random https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/dqrng/vignettes/sample.html
- **Open copies** (publisher not usable): https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.06213

### grouping-and-level-coding: linear-scan → A single grouped pass. Take `pw = rowsum(row_weights[ok], m_vec[ok]) / rowsum(rep(1, sum(ok)), m_vec[ok])` with ok = pair rows with non-NA weight; rowsum uses hashing, and reorder=TRUE gives groups ordered by sort(unique()), the same as pair_ids. Alternatively, precompute the pair-row index once (match/split, or a cached .complete_pair_index_matrix) and index it per replicate. For build_combined_cpoisson_data, cache the pair extraction (it depends only on m, w and X_fit) instead of rebuilding it per replicate.
- **Current:** For each pair id, `which(m_vec == pid)` scans all n rows (vapply or for loop): O(n·#pairs) ≈ O(n²/2), with R per-iteration overhead. This runs inside every bootstrap replicate: compute_estimate_with_bootstrap_weights and fit_weighted_combined in the KK OLS, robust, quantile, cond-logit and compound classes.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by any source. Asymptotically O(n²) → O(n) per replicate, which also removes about n/2 R-level closure calls per replicate. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** This is an implementation fix, not a new algorithm. Same estimator: pair means are identical up to floating-point summation order (mean() uses long-double accumulation with a refinement pass; a sum/count of 2 values may differ in the last ulp). Deterministic, and no new dependency (base R). The na.rm=TRUE semantics must be kept by excluding NA weights from both the sum and the count. The catalog rated the globals.R row 'Keep (trivial)'. The new evidence is the call graph: it sits in the replicate loop of 6+ classes, so it is a hot path, not a once-per-call helper.
- **Suggested action:** Replace the vapply/which loop in kk_pair_and_reservoir_bootstrap_weights with a rowsum-based (or cached index) aggregation. Cache the pair extraction in build_combined_cpoisson_data across replicates. Test that the results equal the old ones to 1e-12.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/globals.R::kk_pair_and_reservoir_bootstrap_weights`, `R/EDI/R/inference_count_KK_cond_poisson.R::build_combined_cpoisson_data`
- **Sources:** https://stat.ethz.ch/R-manual/R-devel/library/base/html/rowsum.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=collapse

### hodges-lehmann-estimation: streaming-moments → Exact closed forms from per-group sums of centered data. Pairwise differences: N/(N−1)·(var_pop(T)+var_pop(C)) with N=n_T n_C. Walsh averages: the sum is (m+1)S/2 and the sum of squares is ((m+2)Q+S²)/4, with S=Σd and Q=Σd² over centered d, divided by M=m(m+1)/2. Centering also avoids the cancellation that the naive sum/sum² formula suffers.
- **Current:** An explicit double loop over all n_T·n_C pairwise differences, or all m(m+1)/2 Walsh averages, with a one-pass sum and sum² to get the variance.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by a source. Algebraically O(n_T n_C)→O(n_T+n_C) and O(m²)→O(m). arXiv 2511.08223 states the linear-time scalar-sum rewrite of all-pairs squared differences. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: high)
- **Trade-offs:** Identical to the loop up to floating-point rounding, and more stable once centered. Low priority, because the only caller is the legacy IVWC path. Fixing the SE scale (F7/I2) is the more important change to these functions.
- **Suggested action:** No new decision needed. When catalog Incidental 5 (TODO 5) is picked up, replace both loops with the centered O(n) forms, and test them against the loop to 1e-12 relative, including a fixture with a large offset.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_wilcox_hl.cpp::estimate_hl_ssq_rank_sum`, `R/EDI/src/fast_wilcox_hl.cpp::estimate_hl_ssq_signed_rank`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.08223

### interval-censored-npmle: icm-npmle → Profile-likelihood covariance for beta, as in icsp2::vcov.ic_sp2 (types oim_curvature, oim_fixed or opg_fixed). The baseline is re-fitted with beta held fixed at perturbed values (profile_fit, warm-started from the fitted baseline), and the information is a finite difference of profile log-likelihoods. Either use icsp2 (LGPL, GitHub/r-universe only, not on CRAN), or port its roughly 100-line vcov/profile_fit logic around icenReg's fitter.
- **Current:** icenReg::ic_sp(model='ph', bs_samples=200): the SE comes from 200 internal bootstrap refits, each a full ICM+Newton fit, using the global RNG.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not benchmarked by the source. The refit count comes from the code: for k coefficients, oim needs k + k(k+1)/2 warm-started profile refits and opg needs k, against 200 cold bootstrap refits. For EDI's single treatment coefficient that is 2 refits instead of 200, so roughly 100× fewer fits, and each profile refit is cheaper because beta is fixed and the baseline is warm-started. This is theoretical, not measured. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** The point estimate is unchanged, but the SE estimator changes from bootstrap to profile-likelihood observed information. Both are asymptotically valid (Zeng, Mao & Lin 2016), but they differ in finite samples, so A/B the SE and CI coverage. The perturbation size h_n is a tuning choice (curvature-based or fixed/sqrt(n)), and large h can fail to converge. Robustness gain: no RNG draws, so the SE is deterministic regardless of seed state (the catalog flags the current bootstrap for this). icsp2 is a 0.x development package and not on CRAN, so porting the logic is safer than depending on it. Its license, LGPL (>= 2, < 3), is GPL-3 compatible.
- **Suggested action:** Add icsp2's profile-likelihood vcov to the catalog's ic_sp SE options row as an available implementation, which removes the 'requires a profile maximiser' obstacle. Prototype it with A/B against bs_samples=200 SEs on fixtures. If accepted, compute SEs with it and keep bootstrap as an option.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_coxph.R::generate_mod_icen`
- **Sources:** https://igrave.github.io/icsp2/ https://igrave.r-universe.dev/api/packages/icsp2 https://www.jstatsoft.org/article/view/v081i12 https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/icenReg/NEWS

### interval-censored-npmle: turnbull-em → icenReg::ic_np: EMICM in C++ (RcppEigen), with a weights= argument since 2.0.8; icenReg is already an EDI dependency. Skip the intermediate icfit(initfit='initEMICM') step. (Re-reported from the previous result; still stands. icenReg 2.0.16 has had no change since then.) SQUAREM-accelerated EM, which this run found, is an alternative. It is about 17× fewer EM evaluations and competitive with EM-ICM, but it is not better than ic_np's C++ EMICM.
- **Current:** interval::icfit(L,R): pure self-consistency EM in R from a uniform start (eps 1e-6, maxit 10000), two fits per arm-difference call, inside resampling loops.
- **Claimed speedup:** icenReg JSS paper, Table 1, from the previous run: ic_np 0.001 s at n=100 and 0.003 s at n=1,000, against Icens::EMICM 0.106 s and 8.3 s. The gain over interval::icfit's EM is not quantified directly. Du & Varadhan (2020) report that SQUAREM averages about 17× fewer EM evaluations than plain EM on interval censoring. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** The NPMLE mass allocation can be non-unique within Turnbull intervals, and ic_np converges to a different tolerance, so median and RMST must be A/B'd against icfit. The weights= argument also unblocks the interval-censored Bayesian bootstrap.
- **Suggested action:** Unchanged from the previous result: prototype icenReg::ic_np first under v1.0.5 TODO-28 (release_v1_1_0.md TODO-50), with an A/B harness on median and RMST, and deprioritize initEMICM.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/helper_inference_survival_turnbull.R::turnbull_npmle_group_stat`, `R/EDI/R/helper_inference_survival_turnbull.R::turnbull_npmle_stat_diff`
- **Sources:** https://www.jstatsoft.org/article/view/v081i12 https://cran.r-project.org/package=icenReg https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/icenReg/NEWS https://www.jstatsoft.org/v092/i07

### interval-censored-npmle: turnbull-em → (a) Interim: compute the pooled icfit once and pass it as icFIT= to each per-draw ictest, with method='pclt' on estimate-only calls. (b) Full v1.0.5 TODO-11 Adopt: get the scores once via interval::wlr_trafo(icFIT=pooled), then compute the per-draw statistic as a linear form in w. (Re-reported; still stands. interval is still 1.1-1.0.)
- **Current:** The generic randomization loop calls interval::ictest() per draw, re-solving the identical pooled NPMLE r times. At n ≤ 20 it also computes a discarded exact p-value per draw.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by the source. The interval documentation says icFIT is 'for increased computation speed'. The theoretical estimate is about r× fewer NPMLE solves. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** ictest does not check icFIT, so a wrong pooled fit gives wrong answers silently; test against fixtures. Valid only for pooled-NPMLE score statistics (logrank1, wmw), not for per-arm functionals.
- **Suggested action:** Implement within v1.0.5 TODO-11. If it slips, ship the interim icFIT= plus method='pclt' change first.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_log_rank.R::compute_shared_icen`, `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_gehan_wilcox.R::compute_shared_icen`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/package=interval https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/interval/interval.pdf

### jackknife-replicates: jackknife → Exact closed-form delete-one-unit/cluster replicates for least-squares estimators: β^(g) = (X'X − X_g'X_g)⁻¹(X'y − X_g'y_g). The per-unit cross-products are formed once in O(n·k²), followed by G k×k solves (MacKinnon, Nielsen & Webb 2023, Eq. 15, proved via Woodbury). For a mean difference this reduces to O(n) arm-sum updates. The weighted (Bayesian-BCa) variant is the same identity with weights. Keep the refit loop for nonlinear fits.
- **Current:** n_units (or G cluster/pair/matched-set) full R6 refits of the estimator, warm-started from the full MLE, through a reusable worker or par_lapply. This includes least-squares estimators (mean difference, OLS/Lin-type, WLS), which have a closed form.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified against naive re-estimation. The source benchmarks only against other CV3 algorithms: at N = 2^20 its jackknife formula is about 12× (k = 20) to 26× (k = 40) faster than the score method for small clusters (arXiv 2301.04527, Section 4, Figure 1). Against EDI, the saving is the entire refit loop: n_units R6 fits become O(n·k² + G·k³). (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Exact in exact arithmetic. Floating-point differences come from the downdating solve and need a tolerance test against the refit jackknife; a near-singular X'X − X_g'X_g, for example when deleting a unit removes a whole arm or covariate level, must fall back to the refit path or be marked non-estimable, as EDI does today. It only covers least-squares classes and needs a per-class fast-path hook.
- **Suggested action:** Re-reported from the 2026-11-16 seeded result; still not implemented. Prototype an exact closed-form jackknife fast path for mean difference, OLS/Lin and WLS behind a tolerance test against the refit jackknife, and add the option to the catalog's NEW-jackknife-influence table.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_non_param_boot.R::approximate_jackknife_distribution_beta_hat_T_private`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_non_param_boot.R::build_jackknife_deletion_draws`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_bayesian_bootstrap.R::approximate_bayesian_jackknife_distribution_beta_hat_T`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2301.04527 https://arxiv.org/html/2301.04527v2 https://doi.org/10.1002/jae.2969

### jackknife-replicates: jackknife → Linearized delete-one-unit/cluster jackknife (CV3L): from the full fit, form per-unit score s_g and information J_g contributions and their totals. The replicate shift is b^(g) = (J − J_g)⁻¹(s − s_g), so G k×k solves replace G nonlinear refits (MacKinnon, Nielsen & Webb, Econometric Reviews 2025, Eq. 17–18). This is the exact-downdate analogue of the catalog's one-step Newton row, but it builds no per-replicate objects at all. Offer it as an opt-in option for â (v1.0.6-TODO-7 area) and jackknife SE, with the exact refit staying the default.
- **Current:** n_units warm-started nonlinear refits (GLMs, Cox, etc.) per jackknife, including the one used for BCa acceleration â.
- **Claimed speedup:** About 40× cheaper than exact CV3 in the paper's empirical (tuition-fee) example (arXiv 2406.00650v2, Section 7.2); not otherwise quantified. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Estimator change: the replicates are a first-order approximation. The authors report that inference based on CV3L is 'usually very similar' to exact CV3, but not always, so BCa â and the jackknife SE would move. It needs per-unit score and information contributions, which most EDI specs do not expose; this is the same obstacle the catalog records for the infinitesimal jackknife. It does remove the catalog's 'R6 overhead per replicate dominates' objection to one-step deletion, which is the new evidence for reconsidering it.
- **Suggested action:** Add a CV3L row to the catalog's NEW-jackknife-influence table, citing DOI 10.1080/07474938.2025.2515161. If v1.0.6-TODO-7 adds per-unit score and information hooks, prototype the linearized jackknife on the same hooks as an opt-in option, A/B against the exact jackknife.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_non_param_boot.R::approximate_jackknife_distribution_beta_hat_T_private`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_non_param_boot.R::build_jackknife_deletion_draws`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_bayesian_bootstrap.R::approximate_bayesian_jackknife_distribution_beta_hat_T`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/html/2406.00650v2 https://doi.org/10.1080/07474938.2025.2515161
- **Open copies** (publisher not usable): https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.00650

### kaplan-meier-rmst: greenwood-variance → Sort the arm once, aggregate weighted events and totals per distinct time (rowsum), and take n_j as a reverse cumulative sum. Then apply the same cumprod / reverse-cumsum A(t_j) / Σ A² d/(n(n−d)) formula. Ideally do this in the same C++ single scan that FastSurvival's rmst_fast uses for the unweighted case, with weights added.
- **Current:** Per arm and per Bayesian-bootstrap replicate: n_j = vapply(ut, sum(wt[y >= t])) and d_j = vapply(ut, sum(wt[y == t & d == 1])), which is O(n·D) ≈ O(n²) (unchanged at 8b718ae1).
- **Claimed speedup:** Complexity O(n·D) → O(n log n) per arm per replicate. No source quantifies the weighted case. FastSurvival's vignette reports about 30× for an unweighted single-pass C++ RMST+Greenwood against survRM2::rmst2 at n=500, but that measures a different baseline. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** The result is identical up to floating-point summation order. It is deterministic, and no license issue arises if written in-house.
- **Suggested action:** Adopt: fold into v1.0.5 TODO-6 (the same reverse-cumsum fix as the weighted KM median) and change the inventory verdict from 'Not yet audited' to Adopt.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_rmst.R::weighted_restricted_mean_se_diff`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/FastSurvival/vignettes/speed-comparison.html https://gosukehommaex.github.io/FastSurvival/reference/rmst_fast.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=FastSurvival

### kaplan-meier-rmst: kaplan-meier-product-limit → Add a weights argument to the existing C++ km_stat_inline / rmst_from_km sweep: weighted d_j and reverse-cumsum n_j over tie groups, the survfit quantile midpoint convention (which km_stat_inline already implements), and RMST by step areas. Call it for both the weighted median and the weighted RMST per replicate. As a minimum, fix weighted_km_median with a reverse cumsum (TODO-6).
- **Current:** weighted_km_median is an R while-loop that recomputes sum(row_weights[i:n]) at every distinct time and grows vectors with c(), O(n·D). weighted_survival_stat_for_group calls survival::survfit(Surv(y,dead)~1, weights=) plus an R area loop once per arm per Bayesian-bootstrap replicate.
- **Claimed speedup:** weighted_km_median: O(n·D) → O(n log n), not quantified by any source. Replacing per-replicate survfit: FastSurvival's speed-comparison vignette reports about 30× for single-pass C++ KM/RMST over survfit+summary and survRM2::rmst2 at n=500, 1,000 microbenchmark reps, presorted input. That is the source's own claim; it is unweighted and was not measured on EDI. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Weighted KM is identical up to floating-point summation order. The weighted tie-group handling and the survfit median midpoint rule (sqrt(eps) tolerance) must be matched and A/B-tested against survfit(weights=). This adds C++ code to maintain. It is deterministic.
- **Suggested action:** Keep v1.0.5 TODO-6 open (still unimplemented). Widen its scope to a weighted C++ KM sweep shared by weighted_km_median, weighted_survival_stat_for_group and weighted_restricted_mean_se_diff, so all three per-replicate paths go through one routine.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_km_diff.R::weighted_km_median`, `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_rmst.R::weighted_survival_stat_for_group`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/FastSurvival/vignettes/speed-comparison.html https://gosukehommaex.github.io/FastSurvival/reference/medsurv_fast.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=FastSurvival

### kk21-covariate-weights: alternating-irls-theta → Compute the θ score and information sums over the distinct-y frequency table, so D ≪ n special-function calls. This is Anscombe's (1950) trick, used by glmGamPoi's overdispersion MLE, and the same pattern is already in fast_zinb.cpp and fast_hurdle_negbin.cpp.
- **Current:** The θ Newton step evaluates digamma/trigamma(y_i+θ) for every observation in every outer iteration.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified at EDI sizes. glmGamPoi's 6-13× is for its whole single-cell pipeline. Here the gain is bounded by the special-function share of each outer iteration. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Identical answer (exact regrouping of the sums). Little gain when most counts are unique; glmGamPoi skips the table when more than half are unique. IRLS likely dominates.
- **Suggested action:** Profile univariate_negbin_tstat on a KK21 count fixture. Only if the θ-step special functions are a visible share, reuse the distinct-y helper from fast_zinb.cpp.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/kk21_weights.cpp::univariate_negbin_tstat`, `R/EDI/src/kk21_weights.cpp::multivariate_negbin_tstat`
- **Sources:** https://bioconductor.org/packages/release/bioc/html/glmGamPoi.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/fastglm/vignettes/count-firth-fastglm.html

### kk21-covariate-weights: forward-stepwise-selection → One reduced fit per step, then a Rao score z for each candidate, as kk21_stepwise_logistic_weights_cpp already does. statmod::glm.scoretest (matrix x2, needs only the null fit, NB dispersion held fixed) and SAS PROC LOGISTIC's score-chi-square entry (cumulative logit, thresholds as nuisance parameters) are the references.
- **Current:** Beta, NB and ordinal stepwise paths refit [1, x_j, S] for every candidate at every step (O(p²/2) fits) and rank candidates by Wald t.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified in the sources. The catalog's theoretical estimate is about p/2× fewer fits. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Score z instead of Wald t changes the selected order and the weights (estimator change). NB θ is held at the reduced-fit value. Already tracked as v1.2.0-TODO-23 / catalog P3.
- **Suggested action:** No re-decision needed. When implementing v1.2.0-TODO-23, A/B against statmod::glm.scoretest and run the existing KK21 weight-comparison A/B.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/kk21_weights.cpp::kk21_stepwise_beta_weights_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/kk21_weights.cpp::kk21_stepwise_negbin_weights_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/kk21_weights.cpp::kk21_stepwise_ordinal_weights_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/kk21_weights.cpp::multivariate_ordinal_tstat`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/package=statmod https://go.documentation.sas.com/api/docsets/statug/v_023/content/statug_logistic_details21.htm

### kk21-covariate-weights: univariate-screening-fits → Null-model score statistics: fit the intercept-only model once (closed form: μ = ȳ), then compute each covariate's score z in one O(n) pass, as Rfast::score.glms does for Poisson/logistic.
- **Current:** For non-Gaussian responses, each covariate gets its own iterative fit (Newton/IRLS) per arrival, and its Wald |t| sets the weight.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. The Rfast docs say only that it is 'much faster' than fitting models. It removes all per-covariate iterations: O(np) instead of O(p·iter·n). (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Estimator change: score z ≠ Wald t away from β=0, so the KK21 weights and the design rule would change. This departs from the KK21 paper's importance measure. Score tests also need a fitted θ (NB), φ (beta) or σ (Weibull) under the null. The gain is small at EDI's tiny per-covariate fits.
- **Suggested action:** Record as a rejected or optional option in catalog §12 (estimator change). Consider it only if a profile shows the univariate fits dominate KK21 design time and the owner accepts a score-based importance measure.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/kk21_weights.cpp::kk21_logistic_weights_cpp`, `R/EDI/src/kk21_weights.cpp::kk21_negbin_weights_cpp`, `R/EDI/R/design_seq_one_by_one_KK21.R::compute_weights`
- **Sources:** https://search.r-project.org/CRAN/refmans/Rfast/html/score.glms.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=Rfast

### likelihood-ci-inversion: bisection → When brent_ci_inversion.md TODO-2 is implemented, use Chandrupatla's hybrid quadratic/bisection method (Chandrupatla 1997, doi 10.1016/s0965-9978(96)00051-8; the solver in SciPy's elementwise find_root) or ITP with n0=0/1 (Oliveira & Takahashi 2021, doi 10.1145/3423597; CRAN itp, GPL≥2) instead of textbook Brent. A new 2026 option to benchmark as well: modAB, a modified Anderson–Björck method (Ganchovski et al., Algorithms 19(5):332, 2026; MIT-licensed C/C++ code).
- **Current:** Wald seed, then an exponential bracket, then pure bisection in δ to 1e-6: about 20–25 constrained refits per bound. brent_ci_inversion.md TODO-1..3 are not implemented.
- **Claimed speedup:** degenerateconic.com, 15 functions at tol 1e-15: bisection 574 evaluations in total, Brent 260, TOMS748 273, ITP 303, Chandrupatla 178. On the flat roots x³ and sin((x−7.14)³), Brent needs 40/46 against bisection's 17/18, while Chandrupatla needs 17/18. The modAB README self-reports a mean of 13.5 evaluations against Brent's 17.2 (max 56 against 142) on 101 functions. None of these benchmark EDI's p(δ), so the gain for EDI is not quantified. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** The converged bound matches bisection within tolerance but is not bit-identical. All three methods are deterministic. Chandrupatla and ITP bound the worst case near bisection's, which matters for the TODO-174 jump-guard and ridge-fallback cases where p(δ) is non-smooth. The modAB figures are the authors' own, and its paper (MDPI) returned 403, so I could not verify them.
- **Suggested action:** Add Chandrupatla, ITP and modAB to the catalog's row-M options table. Before implementing brent_ci_inversion.md TODO-2, run an evaluation-count test on the score, gradient, Bartlett and count-LR p(δ), including a flat or jumpy case, and pick the solver from that. Keep bisection as the fallback.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/lrt_ci_newton.cpp::pval_invert_ci_cpp`, `R/EDI/R/inference_ext_ci_inversion.R::invert_test_pval_confidence_interval`, `R/EDI/R/inference_ext_ci_inversion.R::invert_gradient_ci_uniroot`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_count_likelihood.R::compute_lik_ratio_confidence_interval_impl`, `R/EDI/R/inference_count_hurdle.R::get_likelihood_test_spec`
- **Sources:** https://degenerateconic.com/root-finding.html https://docs.scipy.org/doc/scipy/reference/generated/scipy.optimize.elementwise.find_root.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=itp https://github.com/Just-A-Random-Geek/ModAB-Root-Finding https://doi.org/10.1016/s0965-9978(96)00051-8 https://doi.org/10.1145/3423597
- **No open copy found** for doi:10.1016/s0965-9978(96)00051-8 (publisher version is paywalled)
- **No open copy found** for doi:10.1145/3423597 (publisher page blocks automated access)

### likelihood-ci-inversion: brent-root → Bring this path under brent_ci_inversion.md (Chandrupatla, ITP or Brent, per the finding above) and correct the inventory row.
- **Current:** The inventory says 'Brent root-finding (uniroot)', Keep. The code calls invert_gradient_ci_uniroot, which calls pval_invert_ci_cpp, which is pure bisection.
- **Claimed speedup:** Roughly 2–3× fewer evaluations on smooth functions (bisection 574 vs Brent 260 vs Chandrupatla 178 in total, degenerateconic.com). Not quantified for log-binomial refits. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: high)
- **Trade-offs:** Same as above. The mislabelled row hides that this path is still bisection.
- **Suggested action:** In algorithm_inventory.csv, change this row's algorithm to 'bisection (test-inversion CI)' and its verdict to 'Keep-tracked (brent_ci_inversion.md)'. Add the log-binomial gradient CI to that plan's test gate.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_incidence_log_binomial.R::compute_gradient_confidence_interval_impl`
- **Sources:** https://degenerateconic.com/root-finding.html https://docs.scipy.org/doc/scipy/reference/generated/scipy.optimize.elementwise.find_root.html

### likelihood-tests-null-refits: multistart → Run one constrained Newton refit with a convergence certificate (max |gradient| below tolerance and a positive-definite Hessian, as ordinal::clm's regularized Newton with step-halving uses), and run the second start only when the certificate fails. This is justified because the basic cumulative-link log-likelihood (cloglog included) is concave with a unique global optimum (Pratt 1981; Burridge 1981), and fixing beta_T restricts it to an affine subspace, which keeps it concave.
- **Current:** Two-start constrained null refit (warm start plus full-fit params nudged by 1e-3), always running both and keeping the lower NLL; this doubles every cloglog null refit, including inside CI inversion and parametric-bootstrap loops.
- **Claimed speedup:** not quantified by the source; about 2x fewer cloglog null refits by construction when the first start converges (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Keeps the same MLE whenever the first start converges with a certificate. It does not apply to stereotype logit, which is nonconcave and keeps its multistart. If the second start was added to hide a solver or parametrization failure, the certificate check is what catches it; A/B test it on the fixtures that motivated the multistart.
- **Suggested action:** Resolve the cloglog 'Investigate' verdict: make the second start conditional on the first refit failing a gradient-norm certificate, then A/B the LR p-values and CIs on the existing cloglog fixtures.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_ordinal_cloglog.R::fit_null`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/ordinal/vignettes/clm_article.pdf https://doi.org/10.2307/2287052
- **No open copy found** for doi:10.2307/2287052 (publisher page blocks automated access)

### link-function-evaluation: power-series → Minimax (Remez/fpminimax) polynomial in s² on [0,1/9], the fdlibm e_log.c construction: s=f/(2+f) with a 7-coefficient even polynomial (Lg1..Lg7) whose error is bounded by 2^-58.45. Same evaluation scheme, with fitted coefficients in place of the 1/(2k+1) Taylor ones.
- **Current:** Truncated 10-term Taylor (atanh) series 2s(1+s²/3+…+s^18/19), s=e^{-|x|}/(2+e^{-|x|}) ≤1/3, Horner, claimed abs error <5e-12.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by the source. It saves about 3 FMAs per element (7 vs 10 terms) at much better accuracy. The exp call dominates, so the speed gain is marginal; this is mainly an accuracy-at-equal-or-lower-cost option. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Speed gain is small. Accuracy improves from ~5e-12 absolute to near-ulp, which also brings the fast path closer to R's log1pexp. The coefficients must be refit for the s≤1/3 domain (fdlibm's interval is s≤0.1716, so its Lg coefficients cannot be copied as-is). fdlibm's permissive notice is GPL-compatible, but only the construction is needed.
- **Suggested action:** Fill the '[cite needed]' in catalog P11 with fdlibm e_log.c. Optionally refit the Horner coefficients as a minimax polynomial in s² on [0,1/9] (Sollya fpminimax), with fewer terms at equal or better error. No urgent speed-driven change.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::fast_log1pexp`, `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::log1pexp_array_fast`
- **Sources:** https://www.netlib.org/fdlibm/e_log.c https://www.boost.org/doc/libs/release/libs/math/doc/html/math_toolkit/powers/log1p.html

### logrank-family-tests: logrank-sweep, sort-and-sweep → Use a linear-rank-statistic fast path at delta=0. Sort the pooled (y, dead) once. In one sweep, compute the pooled null martingale residuals m_i = dead_i - Lambda_NA(t_i) (for Peto-Prentice, the pooled-KM-weighted residuals). These do not depend on w. The estimate is mean(m|w=1) - mean(m|w=0) = S_T·n/(n_T·n_C), using sum(m)=0, and the log-rank score equals S_T = sum_{w_i=1} m_i. For each draw, evaluate it as an O(n) dot product, or for all draws at once as one t(W) %*% m product over the permutation matrix. At delta!=0 (log-shift sharp null), fall back to the per-arm presort + merge already tracked (NEW-resample-presort). This is how coin computes logrank_test: scores are computed once from the pooled data and the conditional null permutes only the group labels.
- **Current:** Right-censored randomization p-value: neither InferenceSurvivalLogRank nor InferenceSurvivalGehanWilcox defines compute_fast_randomization_distr. Each of the r permutation draws therefore goes through compute_estimate -> fast_logrank_stats_cpp / fast_gehan_wilcox_stats_cpp. Each draw rebuilds the records, does a full std::sort with record_less and a full sweep, plus R call overhead. Cost: O(r·n log n).
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by a source for this exact path. Theoretical: removes r sorts and r R-level calls, from O(r·n log n + r·overhead) to O(n log n + r·n). FastSurvival's speed vignette reports about 25x over survival::survdiff at n=500 from presorting once plus a single C++ pass. That figure is mostly overhead removal, an analogous but not identical comparison. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Same estimator. Sums run in a different order, so results are tolerance-equal (~1e-15), not bit-identical. Deterministic for any thread count, because the matrix product has no RNG. Valid only at delta=0, or when the shifted pooled order is unchanged. Rand CIs are unsupported for these classes anyway, so delta=0 is the main case. Gate: a tolerance test against the current per-draw path on fixtures with ties, where deaths precede censorings, plus a Bernoulli design with variable n_T.
- **Suggested action:** Add a catalog row (§10, NEW-survival-sort-sweep) and a TODO for a compute_fast_randomization_distr on InferenceSurvivalLogRank and InferenceSurvivalGehanWilcox (right-censored, delta=0). Build it as fixed pooled residual scores plus one W'm product, with fallback to the existing path when delta!=0 or general censoring applies. It mirrors the interval-censored v1.0.5 audit TODO-11, so implement both together.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_log_rank.R::compute_shared`, `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_gehan_wilcox.R::compute_shared`, `R/EDI/src/fast_logrank.cpp::fast_logrank_internal`, `R/EDI/src/fast_gehan_wilcox.cpp::fast_gehan_wilcox_internal`, `R/EDI/src/survival_rank_record.h::record_less`
- **Sources:** https://search.r-project.org/CRAN/refmans/coin/html/SurvivalTests.html https://www.jstatsoft.org/v28/i08/ https://cran.r-project.org/package=coin https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/FastSurvival/vignettes/speed-comparison.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=FastSurvival https://arxiv.org/abs/1807.05504

### logrank-family-tests: turnbull-em → (1) Solve the pooled NPMLE once. It is label-invariant at delta=0, so compute the scores once (ictest(icFIT=) / wlr_trafo) and evaluate each draw as a linear statistic. This gives r times fewer NPMLE solves and is catalog Adopt (v1.0.5 audit TODO-11, §10 TODO-5), still unimplemented. (2) Speed up the single solve: start with icfit(initfit='initEMICM'), or use icenReg::ic_np (an efficient C++ EMICM with weights=, Anderson-Bergman 2017), or the constrained Newton method (npsurv CNM/HCNM, Wang & Taylor 2013).
- **Current:** interval::ictest(L, R, w, scores='logrank1') runs on every evaluation, and on every randomization draw through the generic loop (the fast path returns NULL under general censoring). Each call re-solves the pooled Turnbull NPMLE by plain self-consistency EM in R, O(iters_EM·n·J).
- **Claimed speedup:** (1) r-fold, about 500x at r=501, fewer NPMLE solves (theoretical, from the catalog). (2) Anderson-Bergman's JCGS abstract (as reported by search; the publisher page returned 403) says the efficient EMICM is 'significantly faster than other EMICM implementations and competing algorithms'. Wang & Taylor say HCNM is 'the best choice for data sets of any size'. Du & Varadhan report SQUAREM using about 15-17x fewer EM evaluations than plain EM, competitive with EM-ICM. No source benchmarks these at EDI's n. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** (1) is the same statistic. It must reproduce ictest's estimate and its pclt p-value on fixtures, and ictest warns that a wrong icFIT gives wrong answers silently. (2) converges to the same NPMLE up to tolerance. The NPMLE mass allocation can be non-unique within innermost intervals, but the log-rank scores depend only on the fitted survival at the interval endpoints, so the risk is smaller than for median or RMST. Still A/B the statistic. icenReg is LGPL (>=2,<3) and npsurv is GPL (>=2), both GPL-3 compatible. ic_np's weights= also unblocks the Bayesian bootstrap under interval censoring.
- **Suggested action:** Implement v1.0.5 audit TODO-11 first: pooled NPMLE once plus a linear statistic per draw, in the same change as finding 1. Then A/B ic_np (or npsurv CNM) against icfit for the single pooled solve, compared on the ictest statistic. Cite the Wang & Taylor 2013 DOI 10.1007/s11222-012-9341-9 in the catalog's (f) Turnbull table, which lacks the CNM option.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_survival_log_rank.R::compute_shared_icen`
- **Sources:** https://search.r-project.org/CRAN/refmans/interval/html/ictest.html https://www.jstatsoft.org/v36/i02/ https://cran.r-project.org/package=interval https://search.r-project.org/CRAN/refmans/icenReg/html/ic_np.html https://www.jstatsoft.org/v81/i12 https://cran.r-project.org/package=icenReg https://doi.org/10.1080/10618600.2016.1208616 https://cran.r-project.org/package=npsurv https://openrepository.aut.ac.nz/items/2df2db5c-e028-4874-a724-9988290d3c3e/full https://arxiv.org/abs/1810.11163
- **No open copy found** for doi:10.1080/10618600.2016.1208616 (publisher page blocks automated access)

### missing-covariate-imputation: random-forest-imputation → Re-report of the previous finding, which still stands. Call missRanger(..., keep_forests = TRUE, data_only = FALSE) once, or every k arrivals. Impute each new arrival with predict(imp, newdata = x_new) from the stored forests, using iter = 4 for rows with several missing values. Refit only every k arrivals, or when columns or factor levels change. missRanger ≥ 2.6.0 is already an EDI import, so there is no new dependency.
- **Current:** Once t > p+2, every arrival triggers a full missRanger fit (maxiter 10, num.trees 500, with y included) over all t rows, then a model-matrix rebuild. That is n complete chained-RF imputations over a trial.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified by the sources. Structurally, about k times fewer forest fits per arrival, with only a predict for the new row in between. The mixgb new-data vignette says reusing saved imputers 'is considerably faster, as it avoids retraining the imputation models' (qualitative). (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** This is an estimator change. Past subjects' imputations stop drifting, which is arguably more correct (see the catalog §12 incidental #13). Stored forests use a lot of memory (num.trees × p forests). Per the missRanger NEWS, the predict path is weak for rows with several missing values. RNG use changes: predict takes a seed for its univariate start and PMM, so determinism needs a seed per arrival index. A design change needs the A/B harness.
- **Suggested action:** Keep as the concrete, dependency-free implementation of catalog §12(e) 'stored forest, refit every k' for v1.0.5-TODO-9, and cite the missRanger 2.6.0 NEWS in that row.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/design_seq_one_by_one_abstract.R::add_one_subject`, `R/EDI/R/design_abstract.R::covariate_impute_if_necessary_and_then_create_model_matrix`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/missRanger/news/news.html https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/missRanger/refman/missRanger.html https://cran.r-project.org/package=missRanger https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/mixgb/vignettes/Imputing-newdata.html

### missing-covariate-imputation: random-forest-imputation → missForestPredict (Albu et al.; CRAN 1.0.1, GPL ≥ 2, ranger backend). missForest(..., save_models = TRUE, initialization = 'custom', x_init = previous completed X) warm-starts each periodic refit from the last imputation, which should cut chained iterations to convergence. missForestPredict(obj, newdata) imputes a single arrival with the saved per-iteration models. Re-imputing the training set reproduces the original imputation exactly, which gives a deterministic, auditable path.
- **Current:** Each refit starts the chained RF from a fresh univariate random fill. missRanger has no argument for initial values, so the previous arrival's completed matrix is discarded.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified. The paper claims only 'competitive results … within short computation times'. The iteration savings from a warm start are an inference; the mice literature reports that most of the cost is burn-in iterations. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** It adds a new dependency, although GPL ≥ 2 is compatible and ranger is already used. It is a different implementation from missRanger: it imputes the variables in order, has an OOB-based stopping rule, and has no y-as-predictor handling unless y is added as a column. Imputations therefore differ, which makes it an estimator change. save_models stores every iteration's forests, which uses a lot of memory. It is worth prototyping only if the per-arrival predict path in the first finding proves insufficient.
- **Suggested action:** Note missForestPredict (DOI 10.48550/arXiv.2407.03379) in catalog §12(e) as the alternative offering warm starts and exact training replay. Benchmark iterations to convergence, warm versus cold, at n ≈ 100–500 inside the v1.0.5-TODO-9 prototype.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/design_seq_one_by_one_abstract.R::add_one_subject`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/package=missForestPredict https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/missForestPredict/refman/missForestPredict.html https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.03379

### mle-starting-values: qr-decomposition → Normal equations (symmetric_crossprod, then LLT/LDLT, as fast_ols_internal already does). Fall back to the current ColPivHouseholderQR when the factorization fails, β is non-finite, or a cheap pivot-ratio check flags near-singularity. A start value does not need QR-level accuracy.
- **Current:** The OLS start values use ColPivHouseholderQR (~2np² flops plus pivoting).
- **Claimed speedup:** Eigen dense decomposition benchmark (float, one thread): at 10000x8, ColPivHouseholderQR 36.05 ms vs LLT 6.79 ms including forming AᵀA (~5.3x). At 10000x100, 163 vs 30 ms (~5.4x). EDI's sizes (n in the hundreds to low thousands, p≈2–20) are not benchmarked, and the start is one of several O(np²) passes per fit, so the whole-fit saving is not quantified. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Normal equations square the condition number (Eigen's least-squares page warns against them for even mildly ill-conditioned A). That is acceptable for a start only with the QR fallback. A different start can change the iterate path, and on nonconcave families possibly the converged local optimum, so A/B the converged estimates. Not worth it where the rank-revealing behaviour is used.
- **Suggested action:** Unchanged from the previous run. Fold into performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md TODO-155: micro-benchmark try_safe_ols_solve (ColPivQR) against crossprod+LLT with a QR fallback at n∈{100,500,2000}, p∈{2,5,10}, and measure the start's share of a cold-started fit before switching.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::try_safe_ols_solve`, `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::safe_ols_solve`, `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::ols_smart_cold_start_beta`, `R/EDI/src/_helper_functions_core.h::ols_smart_cold_start_beta_on_log1p`, `R/EDI/src/optimization_starts.h::robust_ols_solve`
- **Sources:** https://libeigen.gitlab.io/eigen/docs-nightly/group__DenseDecompositionBenchmark.html https://libeigen.gitlab.io/eigen/docs-nightly/group__LeastSquares.html

### mle-starting-values: multistart, qr-decomposition → Factorize X_pos once and solve for a two-column right-hand side [log1p(y), log(max(y,1))]. Keep the existing per-column finiteness and usability checks.
- **Current:** Two separate ColPivHouseholderQR factorizations of the same X_pos: ols_smart_cold_start_beta_on_log1p(X_pos, y) and safe_ols_solve(X_pos, log(max(y,1))). Each pays the full O(np²) factorization.
- **Claimed speedup:** Saves one of two O(np²) QR factorizations in the start-grid construction (~2x on that step). The factorization dominates a solve per the Eigen benchmark. Whole-fit impact is small, not quantified. Applies on every cold hurdle-NB fit, including cold-started resampling replicates. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** None statistical: the same deterministic factorization gives bit-identical starts. Minor code change. The gain is constant-factor and small, so bundle it with other start-code edits rather than ship it alone.
- **Suggested action:** Trivial refactor. Add a try_safe_ols_solve overload taking a MatrixXd RHS, use it in make_truncated_negbin_candidate_starts, and check that the hurdle-NB fixtures are bit-identical.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/src/fast_hurdle_negbin.cpp::make_truncated_negbin_candidate_starts`
- **Sources:** https://libeigen.gitlab.io/eigen/docs-nightly/group__DenseDecompositionBenchmark.html

### monte-carlo-test-inversion-ci: bisection → The response shift −δ·w_perm is a linear function of the design (w_perm is its trt column). Quantile-regression shift equivariance, β̂(y+Xγ, X) = β̂(y, X)+γ, therefore gives T_b(δ) = T_b(0) − δ and T_obs(δ) = T_obs(0) − δ exactly. Draw the permutations once (CRN), fit B rq models once, and p_R(δ) = mean(|T_b(0)−δ| ≥ |T_obs(0)−δ|) becomes a closed-form step function with breakpoints (T_b(0)+T_obs(0))/2. This reuses closed_form_ci_from_affine_null_draws with c_b = 1. The same algebra also shows a validity issue. As δ→+∞, p_R → Pr(T_b ≤ T_obs), and as δ→−∞, p_R → Pr(T_b ≥ T_obs); one of these is ≳ 0.5, so p_R never falls below α on that side. When m = 0 (Bernoulli; all subjects in the reservoir) the combined p equals p_R, so bisection ends at the bracket edge. The standard test would subtract δ·w_obs before permuting, and that form is not affine.
- **Current:** Each bisection step of the Zhang quantile CI refits nsim_rand quantile regressions for the reservoir component: rq(ty − δ·w_perm ~ [1, w_perm, X]), with fresh permutations at every δ. The statistic is the trt coefficient.
- **Claimed speedup:** Derived from the code, not measured. The reservoir component falls from about (number of bisection steps, 10–50) × nsim_rand rq fits per bound to nsim_rand fits in total, roughly 20–100× on that component. The matched-pairs component (sign flips with intercept rq) is unchanged. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** This is mostly a correctness/robustness finding. The shortcut reproduces the current statistic exactly (up to rq non-uniqueness and tie-breaking), but that statistic appears not to define a valid test of H0: effect = δ. If the test is fixed to use the observed assignment (ty − δ·w_r, then permute w), the affine identity is lost. The speedup then needs Glazer–Stark CRN or a parametric-LP path in δ instead. CRN also changes the MC realisation.
- **Suggested action:** Investigate first as a possible validity bug (the reservoir p-value does not vanish for large |δ|; check the m=0 Bernoulli path with a unit test). Then either fix the null adjustment to the observed w, or, if the current form is intended, replace the per-δ refits with the one-shot affine breakpoint inversion.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_all_KK_quantile_regr_ivwc_abstract.R::compute_rand_pval_reservoir`, `R/EDI/R/inference_ext_quantile_rand_ci.R::ci_exact_zhang_combined`, `R/EDI/R/inference_helpers_zhang.R::zhang_bisect_ci_boundary`
- **Sources:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantile_regression https://cran.r-project.org/package=quantreg https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.05238

### monte-carlo-test-inversion-ci: bisection → Exact jump-point inversion for studentized statistics. Each draw's pivot equation t(z_π, Y+δθ) = t_obs reduces to a quadratic in δ with explicit roots (Zhu & Liu 2024; Xu 2025 generalizes this to rational statistics). Sort the ≤ 2B roots and sweep ±1/B steps to get the exact p-curve.
- **Current:** Studentized and symmetric-percentile-t BRT pivots fall back to doubling plus bisection on a CRN step function (about 10–20 evaluations × ≤ B refits).
- **Claimed speedup:** Zhu & Liu (Biometrics rejoinder) report about 23 s per two-sided interval versus about 4 h for grid inversion (n=100, 10^4 MC draws), about 0.3 s at n=8 exhaustively, and about 146 s at n=10,000. Xu claims an order of magnitude over grid search without timings. Neither compares against bisection, so versus EDI the gain is about (10–20 evaluations × B refits) → B coefficient extractions. (applies at EDI's sizes: unknown; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Applies only where the numerator is affine and the SE is a square root of a quadratic in δ (OLS / mean-diff / Lin). It handles non-monotone p correctly (an advantage over bisection). The R code is attached to the Biometrics paper with no license stated, so it cannot be reused; implement from the paper.
- **Suggested action:** Re-look (updated): when checking whether the studentized BRT pivots are rational in δ, use Zhu & Liu's explicit quadratic roots as the template. If they are, prototype quadratic breakpoints in closed_form_ci_from_affine_null_draws.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand_bootstrap_ci.R::compute_rand_bootstrap_confidence_interval`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand_bootstrap_ci.R::invert_rand_bootstrap_test_bisection`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/html/2502.18666v1 https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18666 https://doi.org/10.1093/biomtc/ujae052 https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.14024

### monte-carlo-test-inversion-ci: bisection → Keep the fixed-sample (CRN) bisection, made conservative as in Glazer & Stark: p = (1+#)/(1+N), with the endpoint taken on the rejecting side. This argues against prioritizing Robbins–Monro.
- **Current:** Bracketing + bisection; the Garthwaite–Buckland Robbins–Monro driver is planned (v1.2.0-TODO-21).
- **Claimed speedup:** Glazer & Stark (lizard data, n=30, N=6000): 0.33 s versus 0.47 s for Garthwaite (about 1.4×), and conservative rather than approximate. Not measured for non-affine refit statistics. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: yes; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Not a new algorithm; this re-prioritizes a plan. Robbins–Monro gives up conservativeness and start-independence.
- **Suggested action:** Re-check the premise of garthwaite_buckland_ci_search.md (v1.2.0-TODO-21) against Glazer & Stark 2025. Narrow it to refit-heavy, non-affine statistics or downgrade it.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_rand_bootstrap_ci.R::invert_rand_bootstrap_test_bisection`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_abstract_param_boot.R::compute_lik_ratio_bootstrap_confidence_interval`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.05238 https://cran.r-project.org/package=permutest

### monte-carlo-test-inversion-ci: bisection → Glazer–Stark fixed-sample inversion: one sign-flip/permutation matrix per CI search, with the conservative p. Their Theorem 1 gives coverage ≥ 1−α, and bisection then runs on a deterministic function (catalog TODO-6a).
- **Current:** Fresh sign flips and permutations at every δ (no CRN). The p-value is mean(|T_b| ≥ |T_obs|), not the conservative (1+#)/(1+N).
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified for quantile regression. The number of evaluations stays about the same, but steps spent resolving MC noise are removed and the search can stop once the bracket holds a single jump. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: no; confidence: medium)
- **Trade-offs:** Changes the MC realisation. The conservative p-value changes the reported numbers slightly.
- **Suggested action:** Use Glazer & Stark as the reference when prototyping catalog TODO-6a, together with the reservoir-equivariance finding above.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_ext_quantile_rand_ci.R::ci_exact_zhang_combined`, `R/EDI/R/inference_all_KK_quantile_regr_ivwc_abstract.R::compute_rand_pval_matched_pairs`
- **Sources:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.05238 https://cran.r-project.org/package=permutest

### monte-carlo-test-inversion-ci: bisection → ITP with n0=0 (Oliveira & Takahashi 2021). It never needs more evaluations than bisection and converges superlinearly when the crossing lies inside a smooth piece.
- **Current:** Bisection (≤50 steps) on the Zhang exact combined p(δ). That p is piecewise smooth with jumps, not a pure step function.
- **Claimed speedup:** Not quantified for this problem. Expected about 20 → 8–10 evaluations per bound when the crossing is smooth, and never worse. Each evaluation costs only O(d+S), so the gain is small. (applies at EDI's sizes: yes; keeps the estimator: unknown; confidence: low)
- **Trade-offs:** Precision gain only. Bounds may differ within tolerance. For TOMS748 and Chandrupatla, no worst-case guarantee was found (lmi tests show more evaluations than bisection).
- **Suggested action:** Correct the 'step function' rationale in the inventory. Optionally record ITP (n0=0) in the options table; low priority.
- **Affects:** `R/EDI/R/inference_helpers_zhang.R::zhang_bisect_ci_boundary`
- **Sources:** https://cran.r-project.org/package=itp

### negbin-glm-mle: alternating-irls-theta → Route the fallback through EDI's own C++ NB2 MLE (fast_neg_bin_cpp, no new dependency), or through fastglm::fastglm_nb (fastglm 0.1.4, GPL (>= 2)). fastglm_nb runs the same IRLS/theta alternation in C++ with an inner Brent search for theta and a moment-based start.
- **Current:** MASS::glm.nb (R-level alternating IRLS for beta, theta.ml Newton for theta) on the KK21 stepwise fallback path; drops the last column on error
- **Claimed speedup:** Source claim, not measured here. The fastglm benchmarks vignette reports median 1.62 ms (fastglm_nb) vs 7.71 ms (glm.nb) at n=1000 with an intercept plus 3 covariates (about 4.8x), and 13.2 vs 63.8 ms at n=10^4. The convergence vignette reports 200/200 vs 165/200 converged at n=200, p=9, theta=1; fits that both methods completed agree to max |Δloglik| 1.5e-6.
