- link-function-evaluation: `stable-branch-evaluation`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). plogis_safe/array, log1pexp_safe/array and fast_log1p_arr (Goldberg) cost one exp plus a log or divide per element: closed form, nothing faster that is exact. R's nmath log1pexp uses cutoffs at 18/33.3 (log1p(exp x), x+exp(-x), x), and LogExpFunctions.jl follows Mächler 2012. Neither is faster. Boost.Math's log1p docs warn that Goldberg's log(u)·z/(u−1) identity 'fails quite spectacularly' under aggressive compiler optimizations. EDI's configure adds -O3/-march=native but not -ffast-math, so it is safe as built; keep it that way. log_one_minus_exp_neg_hp (fast_hurdle_poisson_glmm.cpp:76) is still not the Mächler log1mexp switch: it uses log1p(-e^{-λ}) for λ≥1e-10, which loses relative precision for λ≲ln2. The fix costs the same and is already recorded as an incidental accuracy fix, not a speed issue.
- logrank-family-tests: `martingale-residuals`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Trivial: a closed form from the null fit, O(n) after the sort. Replacing coxph(~1, weights) with a C++ weighted sweep is already recorded as implementation-only (Keep). No new evidence since 2026-11-16. Their label-invariance under permutation is what finding 1 relies on, but that changes logrank-sweep, not this method.
- matched-pair-reservoir-estimators: `closed-form`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Closed-form O(n) mean/var moments (compute_reservoir_and_match_statistics); reading the data is the lower bound. Rechecked 2026-11-16: no new code commits, nothing new.
- matched-pair-reservoir-estimators: `inverse-variance-weighting`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). IVW of pair and reservoir estimates is an O(1) combination after O(n) or O(np^2) fits; nothing faster. New 2025 work (von Davier arXiv 2503.22080) refines the Satterthwaite effective df for weighted variance sums. That is an inference/accuracy change, not a speedup, so no finding. No code commits since the last run.
- matched-pair-reservoir-estimators: `linear-scan`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). The pair/reservoir split, gather and summary passes (compute_zhang_match_data_cpp, compute_matching_lin_*_cpp, compute_bootstrap_matching_stats_cpp) are single O(np) or O(n) passes, which equals the cost of reading the data. The X part is already cached per m_vec. No new algorithm in the 2025-2026 matched-pair literature changes this.
- matched-pair-reservoir-estimators: `pairs-of-pairs-variance`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Per-replicate BRS λ² is O(n), the lower bound. New since the last run: graph-Laplacian variance estimators (arXiv 2608.10177, Aug 2026), with the complete-graph (Imai 2008) estimator as minimax-optimal. Using it would avoid the one-off O(m^3) blossom pairing (row G, not this problem's entries), but it is an estimator change, not a speedup. Also new: Bai et al. arXiv 2503.10851 (paired-strata design-based estimator) and Chen & Li arXiv 2602.18383. Neither offers a faster way to get the same estimate.
- matched-pair-reservoir-estimators: `streaming-moments`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). One-pass Σx/Σx² in kk_compound_distr_parallel.cpp is O(Bn). The accuracy fix (shifted one-pass, Chan-Golub-LeVeque) is already Adopt in catalog §7 P7 / TODO-4, and Welford was already rejected as slower in SIMD loops. Searches turned up only blogs reconfirming this, plus pairwise/blocked summation (FABsum), which brings no speed gain. kk_bootstrap_reservoir_stats.cpp is dead code.
- mle-convergence-safeguards: `boundary-reduced-refit`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Both entries, fit_zinb_zi_boundary_fallback and fit_zip_reduced_fallback, cost one extra L-BFGS fit and run only on the boundary path. The way to remove them is the α=1/θ L-BFGS-B reparametrization already tracked in catalog §3 TODO 4, and that is a robustness change, not a speedup. Checked for new work since 2026-11-16 (the previous run): an arXiv listing on ZINB boundaries, a web search on the α=1/θ Poisson limit, and the CRAN pages of pscl (1.5.9, 2024) and glmmTMB (1.1.15.2, 2026-09-29). glmmTMB's approach to a dispersion or zero-inflation parameter at the boundary is to flag it and suggest the reduced model (per the troubleshooting-vignette search result), which is the same thing EDI does automatically. No new method found, and no code commits since.
- mle-convergence-safeguards: `convergence-certification`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). No code commits since the previous run. newton_stalled_at_optimum runs one Hessian evaluation and a values-only eigensolve, and only on the line-search-failure exit. ordinal_newton_decrement_ok is one LDLT, O(p³/3), run only when the covariates look badly scaled. The Poisson-limit and limit-score certificates cost 2–3 objective evaluations. The gradient-norm acceptance threshold and the column scale heuristic are O(1) and O(np). This run's searches on the Newton decrement (affine-invariant stopping test; the decrement squared equals the gap to the quadratic model's minimum), on scaled and relative gradient tests, and the CRAN Optimization task view (only optimflex's AND-combined criteria are new) turned up nothing cheaper that keeps the same certificate. Swapping the eigenvalue-based scaling for a Newton decrement in newton_stalled_at_optimum would change which fits get accepted (a policy change, as noted before), and the cost on that rare path is negligible either way.
- mle-convergence-safeguards: `separation-detection`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). bootstrap_estimates_extreme is an O(B) scan plus one quantile per bootstrap distribution, so nothing faster is possible. New since the previous run: detectseparation 0.4.0 (2026-04-19, GPL-3) and brglm2 1.1.1 (2026-10-07) on CRAN. arXiv 2610.08100 (2026-10-06) shows that a single linear program decides whether a finite MLE exists (overlap) across cumulative-link, adjacent-category, sequential and stereotype models. Eck & Geyer (EJS 2021, doi 10.1214/21-EJS1815) detect nonexistence after the fit from null eigenvectors of the Fisher information, faster than LP. Both are exact per-fit existence checks that would cost more per replicate than the current heuristic, so neither is a speedup. They could be a robustness upgrade (an exact check on the observed fit) and are noted under implementations and proposed terms, not as findings.
- mle-starting-values: `cholesky-ldlt`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Researched in dense-linear-algebra. Here it is only fast_gaussian_lmm make_start: OLS by normal-equation LDLT, run once per fit. Per the Eigen benchmark fetched this run, LLT/LDLT on normal equations is already the fastest dense least-squares solve, and it is negligible next to the LMM optimization.
- mle-starting-values: `closed-form`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Trivial: the iter-0 information X'diag(w)X and the NB observed Hessian are single-pass O(np²) closed forms. The weighted_crossprod reuse and the per-row heap temporaries in negbin_smart_hessian are constant-factor notes the catalog already records (Incidental 13). Nothing faster.
- mle-starting-values: `irls`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Only the GEE independence-GLM starting β. geeasy/geepack-style GEE also starts from an ordinary glm fit (geeasy geelm source). Canonical-link IRLS is Newton with quadratic convergence, so a cheaper start would not reduce total work: the GLM start is itself one cold fit of ~5–8 iterations. No new 2026 source changes this. Checked fastglm 0.1.4 (2026-11-11 CRAN), which offers warm.start only across sequential batch fits.
- mle-starting-values: `method-of-moments`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). The Pearson moment θ start (NB and truncated NB) and the marginal-cumulative-proportion thresholds are closed forms, O(np) and O(nK), run once per fit. MASS theta.mm and theta.md are the same moment-type estimators. I found no truncation-corrected moment start that is clearly better: the searches turned up only generic ZTNB moment inversion and ptmixed's capped moment start. ordinal::clm documents its starts as polr-inspired, the same idea EDI uses. Nothing faster.
- mle-starting-values: `ols-on-transformed-response`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). glm mustart-style starts cost one O(np²) least-squares solve each. Rival software uses the same (glm family initialize, statsmodels starting_mu, pscl zeroinfl default glm.fit starts). One related point is about robustness, not speed: the Weibull start ignores censoring, unlike survreg (intercept-only pre-fit, then the full model) or a Buckley-James/Gehan seed. The catalog already records this (O14 'Ignores censoring for Weibull'), and the fix would cost more per start. The speed of the least-squares solve itself is covered under qr-decomposition.
- mle-starting-values: `warm-start-continuation`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Starting nonconcave joint survival models from marginal Weibull/survreg pilot fits matches standard practice: frailtypack fits three independent Weibull models for illness-death starts, and the Lindley frailty paper starts from a Cox fit. Warm starts across replicates and CI steps are already in place (warm_start_dispatch_policy, chain_warm_start in rand, gated on concavity). Supporting evidence: arXiv 1101.6037 reports warm-started logistic Newton falling from ~13 to <4 iterations, and aquakin warm-starts profile-likelihood sweeps from an anchor multistart. No faster acceptable alternative: the one-step/linearized bootstrap changes the estimator.
- monte-carlo-test-inversion-ci: `breakpoint-inversion`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Same O(B log B) sweep as Xu 2025, Zhu–Liu 2024 and Nguyen/CIPerm (one permutation set; sorted w = (t0−t_b)/[k(1/n+1/m)]). That is the method EDI already uses for affine statistics, and at B ≤ low thousands the O(B²) probe counting is not a bottleneck. Nothing faster for the same exact answer. Extending its reach (studentized pivots, the quantile reservoir component) is filed under the bisection findings.
- monte-carlo-test-inversion-ci: `brent-root`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Only 3 candidate p evaluations in the usual case, and stats::optimize runs only when all three fail. Checked: no new derivative-free 1-D maximiser for a piecewise-smooth minlike p, and the exact2x2 (GPL-3, v1.7.0) validation notes say interval-finding algorithms for such p carry no assurance. The lmi reanalysis says Brent beats TOMS748 on smooth functions; nothing helps with jumps. The concern remains correctness (local maximum), not speed.
- monte-carlo-test-inversion-ci: `exponential-bracketing`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Wald-seeded doubling costs ≤7 evaluations and matches Glazer–Stark and uniroot extendInt. Nothing new since the last review; nothing faster.
- negbin-glm-mle: `analytic-derivatives`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). These are closed-form O(np) / O(np^2) passes. The negative log-likelihood and gradient already cache lgamma/digamma per distinct y, which is the lower bound up to constants. AD (TMB/RTMB) gives the same derivatives with tape overhead; no NB benchmark was found showing AD beating hand-coded formulas. Settanni et al. (arXiv 2407.05824 v2, 2026) argue that the published score/information expressions for the NB dispersion are inconsistent. That is a pointer for checking correctness, not a speedup. EDI's derivatives are already checked against the likelihood.
- negbin-glm-mle: `lbfgs`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). I checked for new evidence since 2026-11-16. LBFGSpp (MIT) states no performance claims. No source compares joint L-BFGS on (beta, log theta) head-to-head with IRLS/theta alternation at n≈50–500, p≤10. The fastglm timings compare against glm.nb in R, not against a C++ joint quasi-Newton. Fisher scoring/alternation (catalog O6, v1.0.5-TODO-5) and the alpha=1/theta L-BFGS-B parametrization (P3) are already Prototype. The fast alternatives that did turn up (a pre-trained transformer in arXiv 2508.04111; moment theta, about 1000x faster) are estimator changes and are rejected under catalog O6.
- negbin-glm-mle: `newton-raphson`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). The joint Newton on (beta, log theta) is an optional path. Searches for trust-region/damped Newton NB variants found nothing new. statsmodels NB also defaults to Newton or BFGS on the joint vector. Kenne Pagui et al. 2022 (arXiv 2011.02784) use alternating scoring with bias reduction, which changes the estimator. Nothing new beyond catalog O6.
- negbin-glm-mle: `truncated-series-summation`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). This is dead code with no R caller. The recurrence-based pmf series is already O(1) per term. Yu, Dousti Mousavi & Yang (arXiv 2306.04798, Statistical Papers 2024) is an equivalent reordering of the same sum. Carvalho, Silva & Moreira (arXiv 2202.06121 v2, 2025) give adaptive truncation rules with error guarantees, which could replace the ad hoc mu+10 sd stopping rule for robustness. That is not a speedup.
- non-bipartite-matching: `blossom-matching`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Same day as previous result (2026-11-16), and no code commits since. Re-checked: Blossom VI (arXiv 2604.20351, v2 21 Jul 2026) still targets graphs of up to ~10M edges, its arXiv page links no code, and it shows no gain at EDI's n (tens to hundreds; m = n/2 pair averages for compute_halves). nbpMatching 1.5.6 (2024-09-25, GPL>=2) is unchanged on CRAN. On EDI's complete (dense) graphs, the O(n^3) Gabow/Galil-type blossom (NetworkX/van Rantwijk, rustworkx) is the right asymptotic class. Sparse-graph O(nm log n) and scaling O(m sqrt(n) log(nN)) (Duan-Pettie-Su, arXiv 1411.1919) algorithms bring no gain on dense graphs with n<=~1000. Greedy and approximate Euclidean algorithms change the pairs: an estimator/design change, not a speedup. designmatch (0.5.5, 2026-02-20) uses MIP solvers (highs), which are not faster for exact pair matching at these sizes. The p=1 sort-and-pair path is O(n log n) and optimal. compute_halves is computed once and cached, so it is not on the resampling hot path. Keep.
- nonparametric-bootstrap-distribution: `rejection-sampling`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Unchanged. The mean-diff rejection loop is effectively unreachable (I10). The g-comp rejection of unusable resamples is a statistical choice: any scheme that avoids it changes the bootstrap distribution. van Smeden et al. (BMC MRM 2016) and the Gelman blog note that discarding separated resamples can bias results, a robustness issue already flagged as incidental, not a speed gain. The new 2026 rerandomization rejection alternatives (Metropolis–Hastings + SIR, arXiv 2602.07613; BRAIN; FastRerandomize) address covariate-balance assignment sampling, not degenerate-bootstrap rejection, so they don't apply to these entries.
- nonparametric-bootstrap-distribution: `ridit-scoring`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Unchanged. The per-replicate O(n log n) ridit rebuild has a known faster equivalent: the O(n+L) level-slot counting rebuild, already tracked as ridit_kernel_level_slots.md TODO-3. Searches (ridit bootstrap, multinomial count resampling, pim/DescTools/Ridit) found nothing faster. The Ridit CRAN package is archived (2022).
- nonparametric-bootstrap-distribution: `value-space-bisection-selection`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Unchanged. X+Y selection theory (Frederickson–Johnson 1982; soft-heap selection, arXiv 1802.07041; cache-oblivious FJ, arXiv 0804.0936) and Monahan's Algorithm 616 (used by DescTools) are the catalog's known alternatives. None has practical benchmarks beating nth_element / value bisection at EDI's n, and the plain HL bootstrap kernel is dead code. The IVWC variance loops are tracked as TODO 5.
- normal-cdf-quantile: `closed-form`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Trivial. fast_log_dnorm_vec_cpp is -x^2/2 - log sqrt(2pi): one multiply-add per element, O(n), at the cost of reading the input. Nothing faster can exist.
- normal-cdf-quantile: `rational-approximation`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). I checked for anything new since 2026-11-16.
- R NEWS through R 4.6.2: no qnorm change for non-log p. R 4.3.0's Maechler asymptotic formulas (DPQ qnormAsymp) only affect log.p extreme tails, so AS241 seed parity with R's Inversion generator still holds.
- Cephes erfc: Boost.Math erfc has a peak error of 0.98 eps, about the same as Cephes, and its docs give no speed data. SLEEF erfc and LitMath (1e-13) are SIMD options, but neither has a benchmark at EDI's scalar per-observation call sites. VDT has no erf, erfc or quantile.
- Acklam: used only for warm starts. Wilson CI exact z is already adopted as catalog TODO 7. No new CPU benchmark of Acklam+Halley vs AS241 found.
Nothing new beats Cephes, Acklam or AS241 for these jobs.
- numerical-derivatives: `finite-differences`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Rechecked on 2026-11-16 (code_commits_since is empty; the code is unchanged). Every EDI entry takes finite differences of an ANALYTIC gradient: central differences cost 2P gradient evaluations (numerical_hessian, the GLMM engine, ordinal GLMM, ZINB, Weibull frailty) and forward differences cost P+1 (Clayton, dep-cens), at P up to about 25, mostly once per fit. Alternatives checked this run, none faster at EDI's sizes: (1) Hyper-dual or forward-mode AD (TinyAD, nabla, autodiff) needs about P(P+1)/2 hyper-dual passes of a templated VALUE function, or P forward passes over the gradient, so it is no cheaper than 2P analytic-gradient calls and needs every functor templated (catalog NEW-fd-hessian already rejected forward-over-reverse AD). (2) Complex step cannot be applied twice for a Hessian; the pracma docs and Ridout 2009 (doi:10.1198/tast.2009.0013) apply a finite difference over a complex-step gradient, which saves at most a factor of 2 versus central differences and needs complex-safe functors (already rejected in the catalog). (3) sparseHessianFD-style graph colouring (Braun 2017, JSS doi:10.18637/jss.v082.i10) cuts gradient calls only when the Hessian is sparse; EDI's observed-information Hessians (GLMM fixed effects plus sigma, ZINB, survival) are dense, and the package was archived from CRAN on 2025-12-25. (4) pnd 0.1.3 (2026-05) adds step-size search and parallel evaluation; that is an accuracy and parallelism tool, more evaluations rather than fewer, and parallelism per fit would conflict with EDI's per-replicate threading. (5) Forward instead of central differences would halve the cost of the 2P entries but makes the error O(h) instead of O(h^2), so it fails the accuracy bar. The one repeated use, the ordinal-GLMM damped-Newton polish (fast_ordinal_glmm.cpp:459, up to 50 × 2d gradient calls), fires only when L-BFGS leaves a residual score above tolerance, and the already-Adopted analytic Louis-identity Hessian (v1.0.5-TODO-2) remains the right fix there. Reusing or quasi-Newton-updating the first FD Hessian across polish iterations is an optimizer change, not a derivative method, and has no source quantifying a gain. Previous conclusions for all five labels (FD Hessian + LU inversion, central FD, central FD of gradient, central FD Hessian, forward FD Hessian) stand.
- ols-coef-resampling: `bordered-normal-equations`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Checked again on 2026-11-16 against the problem-level literature (Freedman-Lane/permuco, PALM, residual permutation test, Zhao-Ding covariate-adjusted FRT, arXiv FWL listing, MacKinnon fast pairs cluster bootstrap). For the permutation kernel the fastest exact form is the FWL rewrite: y_res = M y hoisted, numerator = masked sum over treated, denominator = n_T - ||L^{-1} X_c^T w_b||^2, i.e. O(n_T p + p^2) per draw. That is already planned (ols_distr_kernel_fwl.md, v1.2.0-TODO-9). Batching all B draws through one GEMM/TRSM gives the same flops, and masked sums beat GEMM for binary w. PALM's no-permutation, gamma and tail methods are approximate, so they lose exactness; catalog P4 already rejected saddlepoint/moment fits. For the bootstrap kernel the rows change, so its O(np^2) per draw is inherent. Rank-one Cholesky up/downdates cost O(p^2) per changed row, and about n rows change, so they are no gain. No new source overturns catalog §7 P5.
- ols-coef-resampling: `cholesky-ldlt`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Researched in dense-linear-algebra. Use in this problem (BRT kernel): X[i_b] changes every replicate, so the factorization cannot be reused across draws. Rank-1 up/downdate per changed row totals O(np^2), the same as rebuilding. MacKinnon's fast pairs bootstrap only helps when G << n. The Eigen benchmark (fetched) confirms that LLT/LDLT is already the cheapest per-replicate solver (about 5x cheaper than QR at 10000x8).
- ols-coef-resampling: `qr-decomposition`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Researched in dense-linear-algebra. Use in this problem: compute_rand_bootstrap_ci_affine_coefs (R loop, one qr() per draw with a 2-column RHS). Rows differ per draw, so one factorization per draw is needed; nothing algorithmically faster keeps QR's accuracy. Switching to normal equations plus LDLT is about 5x per the Eigen benchmark but squares the condition number. Porting the loop to C++ (catalog §10 TODO-9, mirroring compute_rand_bootstrap_ols_parallel_cpp) remains the implementation-level win; no new evidence.
- ols-fit: `cholesky-ldlt`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Researched in dense-linear-algebra. I re-checked the Eigen DenseDecompositionBenchmark this run: LLT/LDLT on the normal equations, with AᵀA formation counted, is 4.3–5.4x faster than (ColPiv)HouseholderQR at 10000x8 and 10000x100, so fast_ols.cpp already uses the fastest dense method. On per-replicate use in this problem: get_likelihood_test_spec/simulate_under_lik_null call fit_null(δ) on the fixed X[,-j] for every δ, an O(np²) refactor each time (one factorization plus β(δ) = A·y − δ·A·x_j would make each δ O(p)). That is subsumed by catalog §10 TODO candidate 8 (closed-form CIs, 'Adopt'), which removes these refits entirely, so it is not re-reported. No code commits since the last research.
- parallel-task-scheduling: `list-scheduling`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Unchanged since the earlier 2026-11-16 result: inference_suite.R has no commits since then (last commit 168c2e92), and run_all_inference_fork_dispatch still dispatches in build order (pending = seq_len(n_total)). The best acceptable improvement is still LPT order, and the catalog already has it as Adopt (§11 TODO-5; v1.0.5 audit TODO-34, scheduled as release_v1_2_0 TODO-22), so it is not re-reported. Newer work does not change this. Della Croce and Scatamacchia's LPT refinement (arXiv 1801.05489) only tightens the 4/3 − 1/(3m) bound to 4/3 − 1/(3(m−1)) and needs exact runtimes. The 2025 work on non-clairvoyant makespan with predictions (arXiv 2504.10945) gives competitive ratios as a function of prediction error, not a practical improvement over LPT driven by estimates. Work stealing, OpenMP dynamic schedules and mirai's FIFO dispatcher do not help: dispatch here is already dynamic, one task at a time. An R-bloggers benchmark found that longest-first plus dynamic load balancing comes within 1% of the optimal makespan, versus 151% of optimal for dynamic balancing in the original order. That supports the existing LPT verdict. Implementation caveat: the existing manifest (R/package_tests/ci/test_runtimes.csv) holds runtimes per test, not per (class, method, type) task, so LPT needs per-task estimates, e.g. the fit_secs column of past results. Each estimate should be capped at max_secs_per_class, since timed-out tasks are killed at that limit.
- parametric-bootstrap-simulation: `inverse-transform-sampling`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). No code commits since the last research (2026-11-16). Cox event-time inversion: replacing the linear which(cumhaz >= tgt) scan with findInterval (O(n log K)) is already Adopt (§12 TODO-7) and still the right fix. mets::rchaz/sim_phreg (Apache-2.0) invert a piecewise-LINEAR interpolation of Breslow. That is an estimator change: it simulates continuous times, not draws from the step-function Breslow fit, so it is not a drop-in. Ordinal categorical draws: each observation has its own probability vector, so the alias method's per-draw table rebuild gives no gain. Gumbel-max needs K exponential/Gumbel draws per observation and changes RNG consumption, so it is no faster than one runif against cumulative probabilities (already incidental I16). Clayton pairs: closed-form conditional inversion uses 2 uniforms, against 3 draws plus a gamma for Marshall–Olkin. The copula qrng vignette's general preference for stochastic representations applies to higher dimensions, not to the bivariate case.
- parametric-bootstrap-simulation: `random-variate-generation`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Simulation DGP only, O(n) per dataset. Ziggurat generators (zigg, GPL>=2, claims about 7.4x faster normals; dqrng, AGPL-3) and PTRS Poisson would change the random stream, breaking parity with R's set.seed, and zigg's own page warns of lower quality and shorter periods. No gain at EDI's sizes, where the refits dominate.
- per-arrival-design-update: `linear-scan`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Trivial: which_cols_vary / the varying-column scan in process_subset is an O(tp) scan that stops at the first differing value, which is the cost of reading the input. Nothing faster can exist. Checked: no commits since the previous result (code_commits_since is empty).
- per-arrival-design-update: `qr-decomposition`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Researched in dense-linear-algebra. How this problem uses it matters, because the QR is repeated per arrival in 4 subsets, so I re-checked row-append/updating QR. Re-checked: arXiv 2412.05905 (R-only QR updating) and fastQR 1.1.5 (CRAN, 2026-11-10). Neither mentions pivoting or rank-deficient updates, and neither handles the per-t re-centring/re-scaling that changes every row. No 2026 work on updating rank-revealing QR after row appends turned up. The previous conclusion stands: the practical wins are already tracked, namely dropping the redundant second QR (v1.0.5-TODO-29) and recomputing only when the varying set changes (kk14_incremental_covariance.md TODO-1/2).
- per-arrival-design-update: `streaming-moments`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Trivial: scale_columns is a closed-form two-pass z-score, O(tp) per call. A new preprint (Reichel, arXiv 2605.00247, 2026-04) compares Gram, Welford and Chan-Golub-LeVeque streaming covariance. It finds Gram fastest but Welford uniquely robust to cancellation. That supports the Welford update already chosen in kk14_incremental_covariance.md; it is not a new speedup.
- performance-autotuning: `exhaustive-autotuning`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Unchanged. Each axis has 2–3 candidates on a small family × n_grid. Bayesian optimization (Kernel Tuner BO, Willemsen et al. 2021) targets large constrained spaces, and none of the sources tested it on spaces this small. Racing (irace 4.5.1, 2026-10-29) is already catalog §12 option (m) and tracked by v1.0.5-TODO-12. The saving comes from fewer repetitions per A/B, which is covered under ab-timing-comparison.
- performance-autotuning: `linear-search`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Unchanged. The n_grid has 3–5 points, so bisection saves at most 1–2 points. Probabilistic (noisy) bisection (Waeber–Frazier–Henderson) pays off only with many candidate points. Cost is dominated by repetitions per point, which the paired-difference finding addresses.
- pocock-simon-minimization: `pocock-simon`: no_faster_acceptable_found (updated). No faster acceptable method found. Rechecked against the code at 8b718ae1 (no commits since the last research, code_commits_since is empty). (1) Per arrival: pocock_simon_assign_internal and the replay cores score only the subject's J levels for K=2, so each arrival costs O(J). This is the minimum, because the rule has to read every one of those levels. Possible constant-factor micro-optimization, not an algorithm change: for K=2, G0−G1 = 2·Σ w_j (c0_j − c1_j), so one weighted sum and a sign test would replace the two variance sums. It could break bit-identity of tie detection when weights are not integers (floating-point rounding near ties), so it is not filed. (2) Null draws: each Monte Carlo replicate has to replay the whole path, O(nJ) per draw, O(B·n·J) in total. Exact enumeration would mean up to 2^n paths. The real levers are already tracked: replicate-level parallelism with per-draw seeds (carat uses OpenMP per its JSS paper keywords; EDI tracks this under TODO-149, parallel RNG streams) and Besag–Clifford / adaptive repetition counts (Zhang–Zhao–Luo arXiv 2302.05977; tracked under TODO-156). Neither is new evidence against the catalog rows. (3) Updated vs previous: I withdrew the previous SIGA finding (Kojima arXiv 2608.05527, still v1 only with no code). It replaces the randomization distribution with a Gaussian approximation for power evaluation, so it is an estimator change with no exact-test analogue. EDI also runs no nested-rerandomization power evaluation, so there is nothing for it to speed up. The previous record also filed a finding while marking the method no_faster_acceptable_found, which was inconsistent. New 2026 minimization papers change the design rather than the computation: Tao and Zhang arXiv 2611.08378 (Nov 2026, improved minimization without shift), Zhang arXiv 2602.10760, and Fang and Ma arXiv 2602.22648. (4) R-side cost: draw_ws_raw still calls get_subject_levels_idx once per row (a vapply over the strata columns with a lookup in a named vector). A per-column vectorised match() would remove the per-row R overhead. That is catalog incidental #9, an implementation issue. The inventory's "rescans all t rows … O(n²k)" wording for ensure_factor_metadata is stale: since the full_scan guard, only row t is registered after the first call.
- random-number-generation: `inverse-transform-sampling`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). norm_rand (AS 241 inversion) must reproduce R's 'Inversion' N(0,1) stream bit for bit. A ziggurat is at most marginally faster: an informal MT benchmark in the chasethedevil blog gave AS241 0.49 s vs ZIGNOR 0.44 s. Box-Muller and the ziggurat both change the stream, and no hot loop calls norm_rand. Checked what is new since 2026-11-16: nothing overturns the catalog P1 row. The alias method and binary-search inversion do not apply, because EDI does no weighted categorical sampling (catalog P2, N/A). Keep.
- random-number-generation: `mersenne-twister`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). mt_genrand, unif_rand (R fixup) and the RRng(uint32) LCG-scramble seeding all exist to reproduce R's MT stream bit for bit. SFMT, xoshiro, PCG and Philox are faster (Randompack Table 7: xoshiro256++ 0.3-1.07 ns per 64 bits), but each was already rejected in catalog P1 for parity and is tracked as performance_profiling_and_upgrades.md TODO-149(c). Nothing dated after 2026-11-16 overturns that. The 2026 arXiv entries (2605.05099, 2605.18227) do not benchmark R's MT. The seeding cost matters only where a fresh RRng is built per draw index; that is covered under seed-derivation.
- rerandomization-sampling: `mahalanobis-quadratic-forms`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Rechecked on 2026-11-16 with no code commits since then. rerandomization_search_cpp already whitens once (LLT, M = L^-1 X'/n) and computes squaredNorm of M(2w-1), which is the best form. The one GEMM-based upgrade for compute_objective_vals_cpp is already tracked in rerandomization_objective_vals_gemm.md. New searches on batched Mahalanobis via GEMM/whitening and on rank-one/rank-two T^2 updates turned up only generic guidance (whiten, then take Euclidean norms) and no new benchmark. fastrerandomize's October-2026 dev notes report GPU balance-GEMM throughput, but that is accelerator hardware, not an algorithm EDI can use.
- rerandomization-sampling: `selection-algorithm`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). A full order() on N scores, O(N log N), is dominated by the O(N·np) scoring. New check: R's sort(partial=) falls back to a full sort when more than 10 positions are given, and order() has no partial mode. Getting a gain would therefore need C++ nth_element (or Floyd-Rivest, e.g. miniselect, BSL-1.0) on indices, with a tie-handling rule. That is still a trivial gain; nothing new overturns the catalog's verdict.
- resampling-refit-loop: `bayesian-bootstrap`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). BB and PB refits are already warm-started at the full-data MLE. New this run: fwb 1.0.0 (CRAN 2026-10-14) refits through glm.fit with the original coefficients as the start, which is the same strategy EDI uses. WBB / constrained WBB (arXiv 2606.04237) and BLB change the estimator or the target. No exact-refit speedup found.
- resampling-refit-loop: `lbfgs`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Code unchanged: params0.setZero() and 'lbfgs' at fast_weibull_regression.cpp:706-713. LBFGSpp is still v0.4.0, with no performance changes; v0.4.0 adds final_approx_inverse_hessian(), which could seed warm starts. New supporting evidence for the already-Prototype warm start (catalog §5 TODO-3): IceCube arXiv 2609.12941 reports a median 3.6× reduction in likelihood evaluations from warm-started L-BFGS-B, though on a different problem. The k-step bootstrap remains an estimator change.
- resampling-refit-loop: `warm-start-continuation`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Sequential null anchoring is the standard continuation answer. Searches turned up only general warm-start evidence (arXiv 1803.08021, which reuses the original solution as the start, and arXiv 2609.12941). The k-step and one-step bootstraps (Davidson–MacKinnon; Andrews 2002) change the statistic. Nothing new found.
- resampling-refit-loop: `worker-reuse`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Permuting w changes the interaction columns, so QR reuse and the wild-bootstrap precompute tricks (fwildclusterboot, now archived on CRAN) do not apply. No exact permutation shortcut exists for interaction models (lmPerm docs, permuco JSS). The recorded C++ batch-kernel Prototypes stand. quantreg has no new release.
- ridit-mann-whitney: `all-pairs-comparison`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). weighted_superiority (inference_ordinal_jonckheere_terpstra_test.R:161-163) still builds outer() matrices at commit 8b718ae1. The O(n+K) weighted category tabulation with cumulative sums is already the catalog's Adopt item (v1.0.5-TODO-1) and is the lower bound. The searches for weighted / Bayesian-bootstrap U-statistics, Fenwick and sort-based two-sample U computation, and AUGUST / Even-Zohar O(n log n) found nothing that beats O(n+K) for ordinal data with K levels. Nothing new to report; the existing Adopt item is still open.
- ridit-mann-whitney: `closed-form`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Trivial: compute_jt_statistic2_from_counts is O(K) and the fast_ridit_analysis_result contrast is O(n). Both are at the cost of reading the input, so nothing is faster.
- ridit-mann-whitney: `ridit-scoring`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Scoring is O(n log K) by binary search over K levels, and the per-replicate rebuild is O(n log n). The O(n+K) level-slot counting version is already tracked (ridit_kernel_level_slots.md TODO-1..3), as is hoisting the per-element reference string compares; ridit_distr_parallel.cpp:85 now caches treatment_reference as a bool, but :91/:93 still compare strings. The new searches (ridit variance, mid-rank / pseudo-rank scoring, the pim, DescTools, Ridit and ridittools packages, arXiv 2025-2026) found no faster scoring algorithm. Ridit and ridittools are both archived on CRAN.
- risk-difference-ci: `closed-form`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Trivial. The unpooled Wald SE for RD and the RD point estimate with the MN N/(N−1) variance factor are O(n) single-pass formulas, bounded below by reading the counts. Nothing faster.
- risk-difference-ci: `wilson-score-interval`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Trivial closed form, O(1); nothing faster. The open item is accuracy: newcombe_speedups.cpp:23 still calls fast_qnorm (Acklam), so v1.0.5 audit TODO-7 is still open.
- robust-m-regression: `sandwich-estimator`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Closed form, O(n + p³), reusing the cached OLS R factor, so nothing can be faster. This run found no new small-sample correction specific to M-estimators. The issues already recorded (no guard for mean ψ′→0, Incidental 10; Koller–Stahel anti-conservatism, catalog O8) concern accuracy, not speed.
- robust-m-regression: `selection-algorithm`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). One median per fit: std::sort below n=512, nth_element above. That is O(n), negligible next to the O(K n p²) IRLS. The only newer selection work found (a fixed-pivot select, QuickselectAdaptive) gives constant-factor gains, which catalog O9 already judged not worth the code at n≤10⁴.
- sequential-biased-coin-designs: `bernoulli-coin-flips`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Trivial, as the taxonomy says: one draw per subject, O(n) per null draw, already in C++. The per-arrival O(t) recount in R runs once per real arrival and is not hot. dqrng (AGPL-3, so only conditionally usable) offers fast sampling but no Bernoulli advantage over EDI's own edi_rng. Nothing new since 2026-11-16.
- sequential-biased-coin-designs: `efron-biased-coin`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). The C++ kernel (generate_permutations_efron_cpp, running counts) is O(n) per draw, which is the floor for writing an n-vector. I re-checked the exact-distribution route: the Hollander & Peña (1986) recursion and the Markaryan & Rosenberger (2010) closed forms. The recursion works over states (t, imbalance, partial statistic), so it applies only to linear (rank) statistics on a lattice. At n=100 that is about n^4 = 1e8 operations, compared with about 5e4 for r=501 Monte Carlo draws. It buys exactness, not speed, and is already tracked under catalog §7 TODO-6. Kojima's arXiv 2608.05527 (Aug 2026, SIGA) is a Gaussian approximation for design-stage power under minimization: an estimator change, and outside EDI's inference use. carat 2.3.0 (2026-09-08) is a C++ rival but implements covariate-adaptive procedures. No code changes since the last review.
- sequential-biased-coin-designs: `urn-design`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). The urn rule itself is a Markov chain in (nT, nC) with O(1) work per arrival. Wei's UD is a Friedman-type urn, not a Pólya urn, so it is not exchangeable and there is no beta-binomial-style shortcut that would skip the sequential simulation. The O(t) recount in assign_wt runs once per real arrival. The speed gap is in the replay, recorded under sequential-replay. Rivals: randomizeR 3.0.2 (2023-09-18, pure R, genSeq is Monte Carlo and getAllSeq enumerates all sequences) and RARfreq 0.1.5 (pure R, DBCD/SEU only). Neither is faster. Oden & McIntosh (2006) give exact moments, not a faster sampler.
- simulation-study-engine: `clopper-pearson-interval`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). `summarize` calls stats::binom.test once per summary cell for coverage_pval and size_pval. The p-value sums dbinom over the support, O(Nrep), and the interval is two qbeta calls. That is negligible next to Nrep×fits. Checked binom 1.1-2 (2026-07-07), PropCIs and the 2025 fast admissible-interval work (Wang et al.): nothing beats a closed form here. Blaker or Wilson would be estimator changes and are not about speed.
- simulation-study-engine: `closed-form`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Trivial. Quantile cut, normal cell probabilities in O(nK) and Weibull means via gamma() are each at the input-reading lower bound. The survival-factor check (incidental #11) is about correctness, not speed. No commits since the last run.
- stacked-design-construction: `linear-scan`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Trivial: build_matching_combined_{clogit,ols}_design_cpp write each output element once, O((n_d+n_R)·p), which is the output-size lower bound. No code commits since 2026-11-16. A problem-level search on stacked pair-difference plus reservoir design turned up only the source design paper (Kapelner & Krieger arXiv 1305.4981) and unrelated ensemble 'stacked regressions' work. No faster method exists.
- stacked-design-construction: `ordinal-binary-expansion`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Re-checked since 2026-11-16 (no code commits). The expansions emit at most 2 rows per subject (adjacent-category) or min(y+1,K-1) rows per subject (continuation ratio), so their O(nK) cost is the size of the output. Rival software checked: VGAM 1.1-14 (vglm Fisher scoring), ordinalNet 2.14 (2026-02, coordinate descent), GLMcat 1.0.0 (JSS 2025, Fisher scoring over a per-observation design Z; no benchmark found), rms 8.2-1 (cr.setup, the same row-replication reduction EDI uses, clustered on subs), glmpathcr, brms cratio, Stata crreg (direct ML). None offers a different algorithm or a benchmark at EDI sizes. Fitting without expansion (structured Hessian) is already recorded as catalog §2 TODO-3 and is not re-reported. For the adjacent-category stacked composite likelihood, the open issue is still SE validity (Mukherjee et al.: amalgamated conditional logistic regression with a robust sandwich variance), not speed.
- stereotype-logit-mle: `newton-raphson`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Newton then L-BFGS fallback (fast_stereotype_logit_internal / _full_internal / _cpp / _with_var_cpp). Rival fitters checked this run: VGAM rrvglm (IRLS, alternating RR-VGLM; 1.1-14 NEWS has only a 'bhhh' option and no rrvglm solver speedup), gnm (Poisson-expanded IRLS/Gauss-Newton with random starts, and its vignette reports 73 of 100 random-start calls abandoned on a nonlinear example), clustord::osm (optim on a cumulative expit/exp phi parameterisation, numerical Hessian), Stata slogit, the elastic-net Adam fits and the de Rooij MM algorithm (arXiv 2402.07634 v3, still dated 2025-01-22, linear rate). None is faster than an analytic-Hessian C++ Newton/L-BFGS at EDI's sizes (n in the hundreds, K at most about 10). LM/trust-region and expected-information Fisher scoring are already in catalog §5 and tracked as v1.0.5 catalog TODO-6 (Prototype). The Gauss-Newton vs ALS literature (arXiv 1910.12331) supports keeping a joint Newton-type method rather than alternating. No new evidence overturns any catalog row.
- stereotype-logit-mle: `multistart`: no_faster_acceptable_found (updated). Stereotype multistart (.stereotype_full_data_multistart, _multistart_candidates, _null_refit_multistart). The likelihood is genuinely nonconcave: the bilinear form, plus EDI's monotone phi constraint, is not covered by the benign-landscape results for low-rank factorisation. The general multistart refinements found (MLSL clustering, METOD early termination, J Glob Optim 2021; KNITRO rule-based termination) are heuristic. They would change which optimum is reported on hard cases and need random sampling, which conflicts with EDI's RNG-free deterministic starts. Rival software (gnm, clustord nstarts, Latent GOLD) uses random multistart, which is no faster. Change from the previous result: its finding (cloglog multistart gated on a convergence certificate) concerns .cloglog_full_data_multistart, which is not an entry of this problem. It belongs to the cumulative-link problem, so it is not re-reported here and this problem's multistart is marked no faster option.
- stereotype-logit-mle: `analytic-derivatives`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Closed-form score, observed Hessian and expected_hessian, O(n K d^2). AD (TMB, Stan, CppAD) is reported as slower than hand-coded derivatives (TMB paper, arXiv 1509.00660). mnlogit-style exploitation of Hessian structure (JSS v75 i03, 10-50x versus generic multinomial software) applies in principle to the sparse B_j blocks, but the catalog already notes 'sparsity = incidental' and Keep. At d = 2K-3+p of about 10 the gain is a constant factor on a step that L-BFGS mostly bypasses at the cold start. Not an algorithmic improvement.
- stereotype-logit-mle: `log-sum-exp`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Trivial: max-shifted log-sum-exp, O(K) per observation, at the lower bound; nothing faster that is as accurate.
- stereotype-logit-mle: `profile-likelihood`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Variance fallback only: 3 warm-started damped-Newton profile refits plus a second finite difference. The profile-CI algorithms found (Fischer & Lewis trust-region, arXiv 2004.00231; profileCI 2025, convex likelihoods only; CICO) target whole CI curves, not a single curvature. The warm-started Newton already matches their efficient regime. Not a hot path.
- stereotype-logit-mle: `reparameterization`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Cumulative-softmax ordered phi, O(K^2) per evaluation, dominated by the O(nK) likelihood. Alternatives found (clustord's cumulative expit/exp increments, Stan's log-gap ordered transform, softplus increments) are equivalent in cost. None is shown to condition better; Stan's log-gap form degrades at ties. No change warranted.
- stratified-rd-variance: `closed-form`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Trivial. compute_cmh_block_se_cpp and compute_extended_robins_block_se_cpp each make one O(n) flat-accumulator pass over y, w and block ids, then one O(B) pass over blocks. That is the lower bound for reading the data, so nothing can be faster. Checked: there are no code commits since 2026-11-16, and the searches turned up no new closed form that is cheaper.
- stratified-rd-variance: `crude-monte-carlo`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Non-blocking branch of inference_incidence_cmh.R get_standard_error: K=5000 design draws, O(nK), computed once per inference object and cached; it is not inside a resampling loop (checked compute_brt_null_statistics_with_se callers). The faster option is the O(n) deterministic closed form Var(y'w) from the design's E[ww'] for CR/BCRD/Bernoulli/blocked designs. The catalog options table already records it, and the row is already Prototype (v1.0.5 TODO-3), so it is not re-reported. Antithetic draws were already rejected because they give no gain for squared balanced statistics. The Higgins-Sävje-Sekhon block variances support the closed form but are not new. QMC and control-variate searches found nothing that applies to discrete assignment vectors from sequential designs. Those designs still need MC.
- stratified-rd-variance: `robins-breslow-greenland-variance`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Trivial closed form over blocks, O(n), cached. Taxonomy note: the code does not use RBG 1986, which is a variance for the MH log odds ratio. It uses the extended Robins (1988) risk-difference variance from Azriel-Krieger-Kapelner (arXiv 2607.05768), averaged per block. New since the last research: Qiu et al. (arXiv 2408.12541 v2, May 2025) propose a unified robust variance for the MH risk difference, consistent under both large-stratum and sparse-stratum asymptotics; RobinCar 1.2.0 (2026-04) implements GR, mGR and Sato. That is an estimator change aimed at robustness, not a speedup, and EDI's design (equal blocks, even allocation, Robins-type estimand) differs from it. arXiv 2610.01362 (CMH vs AIPW variances, 2026-10) also has no computational content.
- subsampling-m-out-of-n: `minimum-volatility`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). EDI evaluates a 9-point grid at B=251, about 2.3k refits of size m<n. Each grid point re-calls set.seed(private$seed), so the grid already uses common random numbers when a seed is set. The alternatives are no cheaper: Bickel–Sakov is similar or higher cost (BLB paper: 'significantly greater computation'); the Jach–McElroy–Politis KS criterion uses the same grid of distributions, with only a different statistic; moonboot's Sherman double bootstrap is costlier. Adaptive BLB's AMSE-plus-stability exponent selection (2608.16892) is tied to BLB. Nothing new since today's previous result changes this.
- survival-frailty-copula-mle: `analytic-derivatives`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Clayton and dep-cens likelihoods are closed form and O(np) per evaluation. I checked again for new work: CopulaCenR rc_par_copula uses pracma numerical grad/hessian with BFGS, which is slower than EDI's analytic gradient. Petti et al. 2022 give analytic score/Hessian for copula additive models (GJRM), the same cost class. The 2026 dep-cens GMM preprint (arXiv 2604.04032) is an estimator change. No faster way to compute the same likelihood. The FD Hessian runs once per fit (catalog NEW-fd-hessian).
- survival-frailty-copula-mle: `lbfgs`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). LBFGSpp: no new release with a performance claim found (latest notes are v0.3.0: More-Thuente, Nocedal-Wright default). marqLevAlg (R Journal 2021, doi 10.32614/RJ-2021-089), used by frailtypack, claims robustness and parallel speed-up, not fewer evaluations than quasi-Newton at small p. Frailty and copula packages (MultiFrailty: maxLik NR/BFGS; extrafrail: EM; CopulaCenR: BFGS with numeric derivatives) are not faster than C++ L-BFGS with an analytic gradient. For the frailty kernel, LAGHQ's closed-form Hessian would make Newton possible; that is covered by the GHQ finding.
- survival-frailty-copula-mle: `multistart`: no_faster_acceptable_found (updated). The previous result's only finding (cloglog multistart concavity) applies to the ordinal cloglog entries, not to this problem's entries, so it is not re-reported here. For Clayton and dep-cens, the likelihood is truly nonconcave in the dependence parameter. Searches found MLSL/clustering multistart (arXiv 2108.13504) and multi-restart practice in copula papers, but no source showing fewer local solves at 3 starts and small p. The 1-D profile and L-BFGS-B options are already rejected in catalog NEW-nonconcave-dependence-mle. Keep.
- weibull-aft-mle: `analytic-derivatives`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). WeibullAFTLikelihood / WeibullAFTLikelihoodGeneral: closed-form O(np) NLL/gradient and O(np^2) Hessian as a weighted crossprod. That is the floor per evaluation. AD (TMB/RTMB, Stan math) automates the same derivatives and cannot beat hand-coded closed forms; this run's search found no Weibull-specific AD benchmark (TMB paper arXiv 1509.00660 compares only against numerical derivatives). No code commits since 2026-11-16.
- weibull-aft-mle: `lbfgs`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). L-BFGS default in fast_weibull_regression_internal / left_interval variant (and the R wrapper). Making Newton the default, with the analytic Hessian (newton_then_lbfgs), is already Prototype v1.0.5 TODO-3 (catalog §5 'Weibull AFT optimizer choice'), so it is not re-reported. This run checked LBFGSpp (MIT; no new release info) and survival NEWS up to 3.8-12 (no survreg changes). The general Newton vs quasi-Newton sources found give no new evidence beyond what TODO-3 already assumes.
- weibull-aft-mle: `sandwich-estimator`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Pair-cluster meat via rowsum of per-observation scores plus crossprod, O(np^2), is minimal. CR2 / bias-corrected variants (clubSandwich, arXiv 2203.02560 for marginal Cox) would be estimator changes, not speedups. No new evidence.
- zero-inflated-count-mle: `analytic-derivatives`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Closed-form NLL, gradient and observed information at O(n·k) and O(n·k²) already use distinct-y lgamma/digamma tables and do the minimum work for an exact Hessian. Checked since the previous result: an AD-vs-hand-coded search found no evidence that TMB, CppAD or Stan AD beats hand-coded gradients on small models; Stan Math (arXiv 1509.07164) reports gradient cost of about 20× a plain evaluation. The expected_hessian functions (ZAP, ZOIB) are dead code. Nothing new.
- zero-inflated-count-mle: `closed-form`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Trivial: closed-form ZIP θ→∞ limit (softplus/log-add-exp), O(n k), one pass; nothing faster.
- zero-inflated-count-mle: `finite-differences`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). The ZipLimitLikelihood FD Hessian is fallback-only. It costs 2k analytic-gradient evaluations, O(nk²), the same order as an analytic Hessian. The FD-vs-analytic search found only general accuracy notes (4–5 digits) and nothing ZIP-specific or faster. An analytic ZIP Hessian would improve accuracy, not speed.
- zero-inflated-count-mle: `lbfgs`: no_faster_acceptable_found (updated). Updated with numbers. The previous low-confidence finding (fastglm_zi SQUAREM-EM as a possibly faster method) is now quantified by the fastglm CRAN benchmarks vignette. SQUAREM-EM is only 3.7–4.6× faster than pscl::zeroinfl for ZIP (n=1e3–1e5, p=2) and about 2× for ZINB (n=1e4). pscl is R-level optim BFGS. EDI already runs C++ L-BFGS with analytic gradients, so EM shows no speed advantage. It also only agrees with pscl to about 1e-5. Keep it as an A/B comparator for em_algorithm_zero_inflated_mixtures.md TODO-5, but it is not a speedup finding. The hurdle-Poisson block-separate Newton/IRLS (catalog §3 TODO-3) and the conditional-likelihood start and α-parametrization (TODO 1/4) are already recorded. LBFGSpp shows no new release (0.4.0).
- zero-inflated-count-mle: `likelihood-factorization`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). The hurdle factorization (logistic plus truncated NB) is the standard method. It is confirmed again by the Stata Journal notes, statsmodels HurdleCountModel (separate fits only) and fastglm_hurdle (independent parts). Nothing faster exists for a separable likelihood.
- zero-inflated-count-mle: `newton-raphson`: no_faster_acceptable_found (unchanged). Block-separable hurdle-NB Newton with count-only null refits. The comparators checked are slower or equivalent: countreg zerotrunc (BFGS), fastglm alternating IRLS plus Brent θ (its vignette shows alternation can stall when θ drifts to a boundary), and pscl optim. No newer method was found.

</details>

Other sections are as the run wrote them._
